English for Work · Health & life sciences

X-Ray and Radiography English

Practical radiography English for identity checks, positioning, pregnancy screening, pediatric cooperation, portable imaging, image quality, results questions, and near-miss reporting.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For X-ray technicians, radiologic technologists, radiographers, imaging department staff.

Open learner book (PDF) ↗

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Pause an examination and verify conflicting information without suggesting an answer.
  • Give sequenced movement instructions and respond to a limitation without coercion.
  • Explain a pregnancy-screening question respectfully and preserve an uncertain answer.
  • Use concrete language, bounded choices, and honest reassurance with a child.
  • Coordinate equipment, patient movement, and responsibilities before a bedside examination.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.

01Resolving a request discrepancyThe request says left; the patient says right+

Your goal

Pause an examination and verify conflicting information without suggesting an answer.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

The request says left; the patient says right

Morgan arrives for a wrist X-ray. The request names the left wrist, but Morgan describes an injury to the right wrist. Radiographer Alex has not acquired any images. Alex verifies identity using the approved identifiers, records the discrepancy, and contacts the requesting team through the department process. This case does not authorize Alex to rewrite the request.

Alex: Radiographer / Morgan: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which side appears on the request?

Answer and explanation

B. Left

The written request names the left wrist in the supplied case.

2. What does Morgan describe?

Answer and explanation

B. A right-wrist injury

Morgan describes the right wrist, which conflicts with the request.

3. What happens before acquisition?

Answer and explanation

C. Verification through the department process

The discrepancy must be resolved through the authorized process before acquisition.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The patient account identifies a conflict; it does not independently authorize a different examination. Verification and request amendment are different actions.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
laterality
The specified right or left side of an examination.verify laterality
imaging request
Authorized request describing the intended imaging examination.review the imaging request
patient identifier
Approved identifying detail used to verify a person's identity.confirm patient identifiers
examination site
Anatomical location intended for the examination.verify the examination site
requesting clinician
Clinician who initiated the imaging request.contact the requesting clinician
clinical indication
Clinical question or reason supporting an examination.clarify the clinical indication
requisition
Document or electronic order requesting a service.reconcile the requisition
worklist
Electronic list of scheduled or requested imaging examinations.review the worklist
accession number
Identifier assigned to a particular imaging examination.verify the accession number
identity discrepancy
Conflict between identifying details or records.resolve an identity discrepancy
side marker
Marker identifying anatomical side on a radiographic image.verify the side marker
pre-examination check
Verification performed before carrying out the examination.complete the pre-examination check
order clarification
Authorized clarification of an ambiguous or conflicting request.obtain order clarification
acquisition
Process of obtaining the imaging data.pause image acquisition
anatomical landmark
Identifiable body feature used to orient an examination.identify an anatomical landmark
request amendment
Authorized documented change to an examination request.record a request amendment
verification pause
Temporary stop while relevant information is checked.explain a verification pause
resolved discrepancy
Conflict clarified through the appropriate documented process.confirm a resolved discrepancy
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Use the approved identifiers and local procedure. An accession number identifies an examination, not a substitute for confirming the person.

C Notice the language

Ask without leading

Invite the patient to state information rather than simply agree with a suggested name or side. Explain the pause as a verification step, not as blame.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Invite the account
Please tell me which wrist was injured.
Identify the conflict
The request names the left side, while you describe the right.
Explain the pause
I need to clarify this before taking the images.
Avoid blame
I am checking the information, not asking you to resolve the paperwork.
Name the contact
I will contact the team that requested the examination.
Avoid leading
Please state your details rather than agreeing with my suggestion.
Keep the boundary
I cannot silently change the request on that basis.
Confirm the resolution
We will proceed once the discrepancy is appropriately resolved.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
While versus therefore
While can contrast two accounts; therefore would wrongly make the patient's account automatic authorization.
Before taking
Makes the verification step precede acquisition, rather than follow an avoidable exposure.
Names versus describes
Separates information in the request from the patient's own account.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question avoids leading?

Answer and explanation

D. Please tell me which wrist was injured.

The open factual question does not suggest the desired side to the patient.

2. What does an accession number identify?

Answer and explanation

C. A particular examination

The accession number identifies the examination record, not every patient or clinical detail.

3. Which action exceeds the supplied authorization?

Answer and explanation

A. Silently changing the request

The case does not authorize an undocumented independent change to the requested examination.

4. Which wording preserves both sources?

Answer and explanation

D. The request says left; the patient reports right.

Naming both sources preserves the conflict until the appropriate verification resolves it.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

The request says left; the patient says right

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Alex: Radiographer / Morgan: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • resolved discrepancy
  • accession number
  • imaging request
  • verification pause
  • clinical indication
  • requesting clinician
  • request amendment
  • laterality
  • patient identifier
  • Acquisition
  1. Alex
    Before we start, please state the identifying details our department uses and tell me which wrist was injured.
  2. Morgan
    My right wrist. The receptionist mentioned the left, so I wondered whether something had been entered incorrectly.
  3. Alex
    The 1 does name the left wrist. I need to clarify that difference before taking any images.
  4. Morgan
    Can you just change it to right? That is definitely where I hurt myself yesterday.
  5. Alex
    Your account matters, but 2 needs verification through our process. I cannot silently rewrite the request.
  6. Morgan
    Does that mean you think I have given you the wrong information about my injury?
  7. Alex
    No. This 3 checks the information from both sources. It is not a criticism of your account.
  8. Morgan
    Thank you. Who can clarify what examination was actually intended when the request was made?
  9. Alex
    I will contact the 4 or the authorized team following our department procedure.
  10. Morgan
    I have a number on my appointment message. Would that settle which wrist you should examine?
  11. Alex
    The 5 identifies the examination record. It does not resolve the side conflict on its own.
  12. Morgan
    I understand. You will still check that the record belongs to me before doing anything else?
  13. Alex
    Yes. We use the approved 6 checks rather than relying only on an appointment number.
  14. Morgan
    What will you tell the team when you ask them to look at the request?
  15. Alex
    I will state the request's side, your account, and the relevant 7 without inventing a new reason for imaging.
  16. Morgan
    And if they need to change the order, will that change be recorded somewhere?
  17. Alex
    Any authorized 8 must follow the proper process so the record remains clear.
  18. Morgan
    All right. I would rather have the correct examination than hurry past a mismatch.
  19. Alex
    Exactly. 9 has not started. I will explain the verified plan once the team responds.
  20. Morgan
    So we are waiting for a 10, not assuming that either account can simply be ignored.

Answers and explanations
  1. imaging request Turn 3. The imaging request is the authorized examination instruction and currently conflicts with the patient's reported side.
  2. laterality Turn 5. Laterality means the specified side and must be verified through the appropriate process rather than silently changed.
  3. verification pause Turn 7. A verification pause is a temporary stop to resolve conflicting information before the examination proceeds.
  4. requesting clinician Turn 9. The requesting clinician initiated the examination and is an appropriate contact for clarifying its intended scope.
  5. accession number Turn 11. An accession number identifies the examination record but cannot independently resolve the clinical side discrepancy.
  6. patient identifier Turn 13. A patient identifier is an approved identifying detail used to verify the person rather than infer identity from the appointment alone.
  7. clinical indication Turn 15. The clinical indication is the reason or question for imaging and helps the authorized team clarify the intended examination.
  8. request amendment Turn 17. A request amendment is a documented authorized change, not an informal substitution made without the required process.
  9. Acquisition Turn 19. Acquisition is obtaining the images and is paused here until the relevant discrepancy has been appropriately resolved.
  10. resolved discrepancy Turn 20. A resolved discrepancy means the conflict has been appropriately clarified and documented before the intended examination proceeds.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Alex: Before we start, please state the identifying details our department uses and tell me which wrist was injured.
  2. Morgan: My right wrist. The receptionist mentioned the left, so I wondered whether something had been entered incorrectly.
  3. Alex: The imaging request does name the left wrist. I need to clarify that difference before taking any images.
  4. Morgan: Can you just change it to right? That is definitely where I hurt myself yesterday.
  5. Alex: Your account matters, but laterality needs verification through our process. I cannot silently rewrite the request.
  6. Morgan: Does that mean you think I have given you the wrong information about my injury?
  7. Alex: No. This verification pause checks the information from both sources. It is not a criticism of your account.
  8. Morgan: Thank you. Who can clarify what examination was actually intended when the request was made?
  9. Alex: I will contact the requesting clinician or the authorized team following our department procedure.
  10. Morgan: I have a number on my appointment message. Would that settle which wrist you should examine?
  11. Alex: The accession number identifies the examination record. It does not resolve the side conflict on its own.
  12. Morgan: I understand. You will still check that the record belongs to me before doing anything else?
  13. Alex: Yes. We use the approved patient identifier checks rather than relying only on an appointment number.
  14. Morgan: What will you tell the team when you ask them to look at the request?
  15. Alex: I will state the request's side, your account, and the relevant clinical indication without inventing a new reason for imaging.
  16. Morgan: And if they need to change the order, will that change be recorded somewhere?
  17. Alex: Any authorized request amendment must follow the proper process so the record remains clear.
  18. Morgan: All right. I would rather have the correct examination than hurry past a mismatch.
  19. Alex: Exactly. Acquisition has not started. I will explain the verified plan once the team responds.
  20. Morgan: So we are waiting for a resolved discrepancy, not assuming that either account can simply be ignored.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The appointment number is not the identity check

A patient offers a booking message.

  1. Patient: I have the appointment number on my phone. Is that all you need before the examination?
  2. Radiographer: It helps locate the booking, but we still use the approved patient-identification process.
  3. Patient: I thought the examination number automatically established that the record belonged to me.
  4. Radiographer: An examination identifier and verification of the person are different checks.
  5. Patient: Please explain what details you need rather than reading them out for me to agree.
  6. Radiographer: We will follow the actual department process and ask you to state the required information.
  7. Patient: There is an old address in the record as well, which may need correction.
  8. Radiographer: We should handle that through the approved record process without ignoring the identity checks.
  9. Patient: Then the appointment message is useful, but it does not replace the verification.
  10. Radiographer: Exactly. We need the correct person, request, and examination linked accurately before proceeding.
The side is missing from the request

A radiographer contacts the requesting team.

  1. Radiographer: The ankle request does not specify laterality, and the patient reports a left-sided injury.
  2. Requesting clinician: Thank you for identifying the discrepancy before acquisition. The request needs appropriate clarification.
  3. Radiographer: I have preserved the patient's account without changing the order independently.
  4. Requesting clinician: That is important. Reported symptoms and an authorized amendment are not the same action.
  5. Radiographer: Could you clarify the intended examination through the approved request process?
  6. Requesting clinician: Yes. We will review the actual clinical indication and document the authorized instruction.
  7. Radiographer: No images have been acquired while the side remains unresolved.
  8. Requesting clinician: Please keep that status clear so the request is not mistaken for a completed examination.
  9. Radiographer: I will confirm the verified plan with the patient once the clarification is available.
  10. Requesting clinician: Then the examination can proceed under the actual checks rather than an assumption.
A leading question gets a misleading yes

A radiographer rechecks the patient's account.

  1. Radiographer: I asked whether it was your left wrist, and you agreed, but you are indicating the right.
  2. Patient: I was agreeing that it was my wrist; I did not catch the side you mentioned.
  3. Radiographer: Thank you. Let me ask without suggesting the answer: which wrist was injured?
  4. Patient: The right wrist is the one I am describing.
  5. Radiographer: That differs from the request, so we need the appropriate verification before taking images.
  6. Patient: I appreciate the pause. I did not intend to confirm the wrong side.
  7. Radiographer: A yes can refer to a different part of a question, which is why clarification matters.
  8. Patient: Will you record what I actually said rather than assume the earlier answer settled it?
  9. Radiographer: Yes. We will preserve the corrected account and follow the authorized clarification process.
  10. Patient: Then a clearer question can prevent a misunderstanding from becoming the examination plan.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The request says left; the patient says right
  1. Read the corrected exchange, stressing left and right without blaming either source.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the verification and authorization distinction without adding an examination.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

An ankle request with a missing side

An ankle request omits the side. Jo reports a left-ankle injury. No images have been acquired, and the authorized team is being contacted.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Jo: "My injured ankle is the ___ one."

Answer and explanation

A. left

Jo reports a left-ankle injury in the supplied scenario.

2. Radiographer: "The request currently ___ the side."

Answer and explanation

C. omits

The missing laterality is the discrepancy requiring clarification before acquisition.

3. Jo: "The team will ___ the request."

Answer and explanation

A. clarify

The authorized team is being contacted to clarify the incomplete request.

4. Radiographer: "No images have been ___."

Answer and explanation

D. acquired

The scenario explicitly states that acquisition has not yet begun.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Resolving a request discrepancy
Original fictional case: Morgan arrives for a wrist X-ray. The request names the left wrist, but Morgan describes an injury to the right wrist. Radiographer Alex has not acquired any images. Alex verifies identity using the approved identifiers, records the discrepancy, and contacts the requesting team through the department process. This case does not authorize Alex to rewrite the request.
Language workshop: The request says left; the patient says right
Communication goal: Pause an examination and verify conflicting information without suggesting an answer.
Language guidance: The patient account identifies a conflict; it does not independently authorize a different examination. Verification and request amendment are different actions.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Please tell me which wrist was injured. / The request names the left side, while you describe the right. / I need to clarify this before taking the images. / I am checking the information, not asking you to resolve the paperwork. / I will contact the team that requested the examination. / Please state your details rather than agreeing with my suggestion. / I cannot silently change the request on that basis. / We will proceed once the discrepancy is appropriately resolved. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: laterality: The specified right or left side of an examination. / imaging request: Authorized request describing the intended imaging examination. / patient identifier: Approved identifying detail used to verify a person's identity. / examination site: Anatomical location intended for the examination. / requesting clinician: Clinician who initiated the imaging request. / clinical indication: Clinical question or reason supporting an examination. / requisition: Document or electronic order requesting a service. / worklist: Electronic list of scheduled or requested imaging examinations. / accession number: Identifier assigned to a particular imaging examination. / identity discrepancy: Conflict between identifying details or records. / side marker: Marker identifying anatomical side on a radiographic image. / pre-examination check: Verification performed before carrying out the examination. / order clarification: Authorized clarification of an ambiguous or conflicting request. / acquisition: Process of obtaining the imaging data. / anatomical landmark: Identifiable body feature used to orient an examination. / request amendment: Authorized documented change to an examination request. / verification pause: Temporary stop while relevant information is checked. / resolved discrepancy: Conflict clarified through the appropriate documented process. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected exchange, stressing left and right without blaming either source. Swap roles. Repeat the verification and authorization distinction without adding an examination. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Alex: Radiographer / Morgan: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An ankle request omits the side. Jo reports a left-ankle injury. No images have been acquired, and the authorized team is being contacted.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: Any authorized request amendment must follow the proper process so the record remains clear.
Morgan: All right. I would rather have the correct examination than hurry past a mismatch.
Alex: Exactly. Acquisition has not started. I will explain the verified plan once the team responds.
Morgan: So we are waiting for a resolved discrepancy, not assuming that either account can simply be ignored.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: Any authorized request amendment must follow the proper process so the record remains clear.
Morgan: All right. I would rather have the correct examination than hurry past a mismatch.
Alex: Exactly. Acquisition has not started. I will explain the verified plan once the team responds.
Morgan: So we are waiting for a resolved discrepancy, not assuming that either account can simply be ignored.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Lesson 01: Resolving a request discrepancy
Original fictional case: Morgan arrives for a wrist X-ray. The request names the left wrist, but Morgan describes an injury to the right wrist. Radiographer Alex has not acquired any images. Alex verifies identity using the approved identifiers, records the discrepancy, and contacts the requesting team through the department process. This case does not authorize Alex to rewrite the request.
Language workshop: The request says left; the patient says right
Communication goal: Pause an examination and verify conflicting information without suggesting an answer.
Language guidance: The patient account identifies a conflict; it does not independently authorize a different examination. Verification and request amendment are different actions.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Please tell me which wrist was injured. / The request names the left side, while you describe the right. / I need to clarify this before taking the images. / I am checking the information, not asking you to resolve the paperwork. / I will contact the team that requested the examination. / Please state your details rather than agreeing with my suggestion. / I cannot silently change the request on that basis. / We will proceed once the discrepancy is appropriately resolved. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: laterality: The specified right or left side of an examination. / imaging request: Authorized request describing the intended imaging examination. / patient identifier: Approved identifying detail used to verify a person's identity. / examination site: Anatomical location intended for the examination. / requesting clinician: Clinician who initiated the imaging request. / clinical indication: Clinical question or reason supporting an examination. / requisition: Document or electronic order requesting a service. / worklist: Electronic list of scheduled or requested imaging examinations. / accession number: Identifier assigned to a particular imaging examination. / identity discrepancy: Conflict between identifying details or records. / side marker: Marker identifying anatomical side on a radiographic image. / pre-examination check: Verification performed before carrying out the examination. / order clarification: Authorized clarification of an ambiguous or conflicting request. / acquisition: Process of obtaining the imaging data. / anatomical landmark: Identifiable body feature used to orient an examination. / request amendment: Authorized documented change to an examination request. / verification pause: Temporary stop while relevant information is checked. / resolved discrepancy: Conflict clarified through the appropriate documented process. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected exchange, stressing left and right without blaming either source. Swap roles. Repeat the verification and authorization distinction without adding an examination. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Alex: Radiographer / Morgan: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An ankle request omits the side. Jo reports a left-ankle injury. No images have been acquired, and the authorized team is being contacted.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: Any authorized request amendment must follow the proper process so the record remains clear.
Morgan: All right. I would rather have the correct examination than hurry past a mismatch.
Alex: Exactly. Acquisition has not started. I will explain the verified plan once the team responds.
Morgan: So we are waiting for a resolved discrepancy, not assuming that either account can simply be ignored.

02Adapting positioning instructionsOne movement at a time+

Your goal

Give sequenced movement instructions and respond to a limitation without coercion.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

One movement at a time

Casey cannot comfortably raise the left shoulder for the planned position. Radiographer Jamie stops the attempted movement and checks the limitation. An adapted examination requires the appropriate departmental approach and, where needed, clinical advice. Casey has not refused all imaging; Casey has said this particular movement hurts. No alternative projection is prescribed by the exercise.

Jamie: Radiographer / Casey: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is difficult?

Answer and explanation

A. Raising the left shoulder

The case identifies one painful shoulder movement, not a general refusal.

2. What does Jamie do first?

Answer and explanation

D. Stops the attempted movement

Jamie stops the movement and checks the reported limitation.

3. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. An authorized alternative projection

The exercise does not prescribe the technical alternative for the examination.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Cannot comfortably move is different from refuses imaging. Record the actual limitation and avoid labeling the patient uncooperative.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
positioning
Arranging the patient's body for the intended image.explain the positioning
projection
Image view defined by the direction of the X-ray beam.clarify the requested projection
range of motion
Extent of movement available at a joint.assess available range of motion
mobility limitation
Restriction affecting a person's movement.accommodate a mobility limitation
weight bearing
Supporting body weight through a limb or joint.clarify weight-bearing ability
transfer assistance
Help moving between surfaces or positions.arrange transfer assistance
support aid
Equipment used to help maintain a suitable position.select an approved support aid
upright position
Position with the upper body vertical.explain an upright position
supine position
Position lying on the back.describe the supine position
lateral position
Position lying on or oriented toward a side.clarify the lateral position
rotation
Turning around a body or joint axis.limit uncomfortable rotation
alignment
Relationship of body parts to the intended imaging geometry.check patient alignment
immobilization
Restriction of movement using an appropriate method.explain proposed immobilization
pain-limited movement
Movement restricted by the person's reported pain.document pain-limited movement
physical assistance
Hands-on support provided with appropriate agreement and competence.ask before physical assistance
breath-hold instruction
Direction to briefly suspend breathing for an image when appropriate.rehearse a breath-hold instruction
adapted technique
Modified examination approach appropriate to the clinical and technical situation.discuss an adapted technique
movement cue
Short instruction directing one specific movement.give a clear movement cue
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

A language exercise cannot determine a safe transfer, immobilization method, or alternative projection. Follow the actual assessment and authorized technique.

C Notice the language

A limitation is useful information

Thank the patient for reporting pain. Avoid interpreting inability as unwillingness, and obtain agreement before offering physical assistance.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Give one step
Please turn slightly toward me, then stop.
Invite a limit
Tell me before moving if that is painful or difficult.
Stop clearly
Stop there; do not force that movement.
Clarify ability
Which part of that movement is difficult?
Ask before touch
May I help support your arm?
Acknowledge the report
Thank you for telling me about the pain.
Explain adaptation
I will check how we can adapt the examination appropriately.
Rehearse first
We can practice the instruction before acquiring the image.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Cannot versus will not
Cannot describes a limitation; will not may imply refusal and should not replace the patient's actual account.
Then stop
Breaks a complex instruction into a bounded movement that can be checked.
May I help
Requests agreement before hands-on assistance rather than announcing it as inevitable.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which record is most accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Casey reports pain when raising the left shoulder.

The precise report describes the supplied movement limitation without inventing intent or examination findings.

2. Which instruction respects the limitation?

Answer and explanation

D. Stop there; do not force the movement.

Stopping acknowledges the reported difficulty while the appropriate approach is reviewed.

3. What should precede hands-on help?

Answer and explanation

D. Appropriate agreement and assessment

Assistance requires appropriate agreement and competence, not an unexpected physical intervention.

4. What is a projection?

Answer and explanation

B. A view defined by beam direction

Projection names an imaging view and does not itself authorize a technical change.

E Conversations

One movement at a time

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jamie: Radiographer / Casey: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • movement cue
  • Physical assistance
  • adapted technique
  • breath-hold instruction
  • positioning
  • pain-limited movement
  • range of motion
  • projection
  • mobility limitation
  • support aid
  1. Jamie
    I will explain one movement at a time. Please tell me before moving if a position is painful or difficult.
  2. Casey
    Raising this left shoulder hurts. I can turn a little, but I cannot lift the arm that high.
  3. Jamie
    Stop there. This is 1, and we should not force the position just to follow the first instruction.
  4. Casey
    I do want the examination. I am worried you will think I am refusing to cooperate.
  5. Jamie
    I will record the actual 2, not describe you as refusing every part of the examination.
  6. Casey
    Thank you. It is easier when I know exactly which movement you are asking me to try.
  7. Jamie
    I can give a shorter 3 and pause between steps. We can check what is comfortable before proceeding.
  8. Casey
    Could you explain what the position is intended to show without asking me to force it?
  9. Jamie
    The requested 4 is a particular view. I will check the appropriate approach when that positioning is difficult.
  10. Casey
    Would a cushion or some support help, or does that need to be checked as well?
  11. Jamie
    Any 5 must be appropriate for this examination.
  12. Casey
    I would also like you to ask before moving my arm, because unexpected movement makes me tense.
  13. Jamie
    Of course. 6 needs your agreement and a suitable assessment. May I help support it while you stay still?
  14. Casey
    Yes, supporting it is comfortable. Please do not lift it farther without checking with me first.
  15. Jamie
    Understood. I will keep your 7 limitation clear when I discuss the examination with the team.
  16. Casey
    Does changing the way I sit automatically mean you can take a different view instead?
  17. Jamie
    No. An 8 must fit the clinical question and our authorized process, rather than be an improvised substitute.
  18. Casey
    Once that is settled, could we practice any breathing instruction before the image is taken?
  19. Jamie
    Yes, if a 9 is appropriate, we can rehearse it and check that the wording is manageable for you.
  20. Casey
    Clear 10 instructions and a pause give me a chance to tell you what I can actually do.

Answers and explanations
  1. pain-limited movement Turn 3. Pain-limited movement accurately records the reported reason for stopping rather than assuming refusal or poor cooperation.
  2. mobility limitation Turn 5. A mobility limitation describes restricted movement without assigning the patient a negative motive or a blanket refusal.
  3. movement cue Turn 7. A movement cue directs one specific action and allows the patient's response to be checked before another instruction.
  4. projection Turn 9. A projection is an imaging view defined by beam direction and its intended purpose should be distinguished from an improvised alternative.
  5. support aid Turn 11. A support aid helps maintain a position but must be appropriate to the actual examination and the person's needs.
  6. Physical assistance Turn 13. Physical assistance is hands-on support and requires appropriate agreement, assessment, and competence rather than surprise movement.
  7. range of motion Turn 15. Range of motion describes the movement available at the joint and the reported limitation must remain part of the positioning discussion.
  8. adapted technique Turn 17. An adapted technique is an appropriate modification based on the actual clinical and technical situation, not an automatic substitution.
  9. breath-hold instruction Turn 19. A breath-hold instruction can be rehearsed when appropriate so the patient understands the sequence before acquisition.
  10. positioning Turn 20. Positioning arranges the body for the intended image, and the instructions should respect the person's actual movement ability.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jamie: I will explain one movement at a time. Please tell me before moving if a position is painful or difficult.
  2. Casey: Raising this left shoulder hurts. I can turn a little, but I cannot lift the arm that high.
  3. Jamie: Stop there. This is pain-limited movement, and we should not force the position just to follow the first instruction.
  4. Casey: I do want the examination. I am worried you will think I am refusing to cooperate.
  5. Jamie: I will record the actual mobility limitation, not describe you as refusing every part of the examination.
  6. Casey: Thank you. It is easier when I know exactly which movement you are asking me to try.
  7. Jamie: I can give a shorter movement cue and pause between steps. We can check what is comfortable before proceeding.
  8. Casey: Could you explain what the position is intended to show without asking me to force it?
  9. Jamie: The requested projection is a particular view. I will check the appropriate approach when that positioning is difficult.
  10. Casey: Would a cushion or some support help, or does that need to be checked as well?
  11. Jamie: Any support aid must be appropriate for this examination.
  12. Casey: I would also like you to ask before moving my arm, because unexpected movement makes me tense.
  13. Jamie: Of course. Physical assistance needs your agreement and a suitable assessment. May I help support it while you stay still?
  14. Casey: Yes, supporting it is comfortable. Please do not lift it farther without checking with me first.
  15. Jamie: Understood. I will keep your range of motion limitation clear when I discuss the examination with the team.
  16. Casey: Does changing the way I sit automatically mean you can take a different view instead?
  17. Jamie: No. An adapted technique must fit the clinical question and our authorized process, rather than be an improvised substitute.
  18. Casey: Once that is settled, could we practice any breathing instruction before the image is taken?
  19. Jamie: Yes, if a breath-hold instruction is appropriate, we can rehearse it and check that the wording is manageable for you.
  20. Casey: Clear positioning instructions and a pause give me a chance to tell you what I can actually do.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Cannot move is not refusing

A patient explains a positioning limitation.

  1. Patient: I cannot raise the arm that high without pain, but I am not refusing the examination.
  2. Radiographer: Thank you for distinguishing that. Stop the movement while we review the appropriate approach.
  3. Patient: I was worried that the record would simply call me uncooperative.
  4. Radiographer: We should document the actual limitation rather than make a judgment about your intention.
  5. Patient: Could you explain one movement at a time so I know exactly what is being requested?
  6. Radiographer: Yes. A short instruction and pause let us check what is manageable.
  7. Patient: I also want you to ask before offering hands-on support.
  8. Radiographer: We will explain any proposed assistance and obtain the appropriate agreement.
  9. Patient: Does this automatically authorize a different projection?
  10. Radiographer: No. Any adapted examination must follow the actual assessment and authorized technical process.
A breathing instruction needs rehearsal

A radiographer checks a patient's understanding before acquisition.

  1. Radiographer: Before the image, let us practice the breathing instruction that applies to this examination.
  2. Patient: I find it difficult when several instructions are given quickly together.
  3. Radiographer: We can separate the steps and make clear when practice ends and acquisition begins.
  4. Patient: I should not have to guess whether you mean breathe normally or hold at that moment.
  5. Radiographer: Correct. The actual instruction needs to be clear and appropriate for you.
  6. Patient: If I cannot manage it comfortably, should I say so before trying again?
  7. Radiographer: Yes. Tell me about the difficulty so the appropriate approach can be reviewed.
  8. Patient: I appreciate practicing without an exposure rather than feeling rushed into the sequence.
  9. Radiographer: The rehearsal checks understanding; it does not replace the actual examination and safety checks.
  10. Patient: Then we can proceed only with the verified plan and a clear instruction.
Transfer assistance needs assessment

Two staff members coordinate a patient movement.

  1. Radiographer: The patient reports difficulty standing from the chair. We need the appropriate transfer assessment.
  2. Nurse: I will identify the relevant assistance rather than assume a single person can manage it.
  3. Radiographer: The examination request does not itself tell us which transfer method is suitable.
  4. Nurse: Agreed. The patient's actual needs and our authorized process determine that.
  5. Radiographer: I will not move the patient unexpectedly while waiting for the assessment.
  6. Nurse: Please explain the pause and ask about the difficulty without assigning a diagnosis.
  7. Radiographer: Once the assistance is confirmed, we can coordinate the sequence and responsibilities.
  8. Nurse: Yes, including who provides support and when the patient is ready.
  9. Radiographer: A clear floor alone does not establish that the transfer is safe.
  10. Nurse: Exactly. Practical preparation includes assessment, appropriate people, equipment, and the patient's understanding.

Two scenarios to practice

One movement at a time
  1. Read the corrected exchange with a clear pause after each movement instruction.
  2. Swap roles. Contrast a painful movement with refusal, and practice asking before assistance.

A movement limitation is not equivalent to refusing imaging.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: X-Ray and Radiography English
Professional audience: X-ray technicians, radiologic technologists, radiographers, imaging department staff
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.
Lesson 02: Adapting positioning instructions
Original fictional case: Casey cannot comfortably raise the left shoulder for the planned position. Radiographer Jamie stops the attempted movement and checks the limitation. An adapted examination requires the appropriate departmental approach and, where needed, clinical advice. Casey has not refused all imaging; Casey has said this particular movement hurts. No alternative projection is prescribed by the exercise.
Language workshop: One movement at a time
Communication goal: Give sequenced movement instructions and respond to a limitation without coercion.
Language guidance: Cannot comfortably move is different from refuses imaging. Record the actual limitation and avoid labeling the patient uncooperative.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Please turn slightly toward me, then stop. / Tell me before moving if that is painful or difficult. / Stop there; do not force that movement. / Which part of that movement is difficult? / May I help support your arm? / Thank you for telling me about the pain. / I will check how we can adapt the examination appropriately. / We can practice the instruction before acquiring the image. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: positioning: Arranging the patient's body for the intended image. / projection: Image view defined by the direction of the X-ray beam. / range of motion: Extent of movement available at a joint. / mobility limitation: Restriction affecting a person's movement. / weight bearing: Supporting body weight through a limb or joint. / transfer assistance: Help moving between surfaces or positions. / support aid: Equipment used to help maintain a suitable position. / upright position: Position with the upper body vertical. / supine position: Position lying on the back. / lateral position: Position lying on or oriented toward a side. / rotation: Turning around a body or joint axis. / alignment: Relationship of body parts to the intended imaging geometry. / immobilization: Restriction of movement using an appropriate method. / pain-limited movement: Movement restricted by the person's reported pain. / physical assistance: Hands-on support provided with appropriate agreement and competence. / breath-hold instruction: Direction to briefly suspend breathing for an image when appropriate. / adapted technique: Modified examination approach appropriate to the clinical and technical situation. / movement cue: Short instruction directing one specific movement. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected exchange with a clear pause after each movement instruction. Swap roles. Contrast a painful movement with refusal, and practice asking before assistance. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Jamie: Radiographer / Casey: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Drew says standing from the chair is difficult. The radiographer has not assessed transfer assistance and asks the appropriate team for help. Drew has not refused imaging.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jamie: No. An adapted technique must fit the clinical question and our authorized process, rather than be an improvised substitute.
Casey: Once that is settled, could we practice any breathing instruction before the image is taken?
Jamie: Yes, if a breath-hold instruction is appropriate, we can rehearse it and check that the wording is manageable for you.
Casey: Clear positioning instructions and a pause give me a chance to tell you what I can actually do.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Asking sensitive screening questionsA private answer before the examination+

Your goal

Explain a pregnancy-screening question respectfully and preserve an uncertain answer.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A private answer before the examination

Rowan is asked a pregnancy-screening question under the department protocol and requests privacy from a companion. Rowan is unsure of the answer. Radiographer Taylor offers a private conversation and follows the appropriate review process. The uncertainty is not recorded as a negative answer. The exercise supplies neither an individualized radiation-risk estimate nor a decision to perform or cancel the examination.

Taylor: Radiographer / Rowan: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Rowan request?

Answer and explanation

D. Privacy from a companion

Rowan specifically asks to answer the screening question privately.

2. What answer is supplied?

Answer and explanation

A. Uncertainty

Rowan is unsure, and that uncertainty must remain visible.

3. What decision is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

A. Whether to perform or cancel the examination

The language case does not determine the clinical examination decision.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Unsure is not no. Screening information supports the appropriate review; it does not create a universal instruction to cancel or proceed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
pregnancy screening
Process of checking relevant pregnancy information before an examination.explain pregnancy screening
possible pregnancy
Pregnancy that has not been confirmed or excluded.report possible pregnancy
screening uncertainty
Unresolved information from a screening question or process.preserve screening uncertainty
private conversation
Discussion held away from people not authorized to hear it.offer a private conversation
sensitive history
Personal information requiring respectful and appropriate handling.obtain a sensitive history
ionizing radiation
Radiation capable of removing electrons from atoms.explain ionizing radiation
radiation exposure
Exposure to radiation during an examination or other event.discuss radiation exposure
justification
Assessment that an examination's expected benefit warrants its use.review examination justification
optimization
Adjusting imaging practice to achieve its purpose with appropriate exposure.explain optimization
benefit-risk discussion
Comparison of expected benefit and relevant possible harm.arrange a benefit-risk discussion
radiologist review
Assessment by a physician specializing in medical imaging.request radiologist review
examination protocol
Approved approach for a particular imaging examination.follow the examination protocol
screening response
Recorded answer to a screening question.document the screening response
exposure history
Account of relevant prior radiation exposure.clarify the exposure history
reproductive history
Relevant history concerning pregnancy and reproduction.ask about reproductive history
privacy preference
Person's stated wishes about who hears personal information.respect a privacy preference
clinical urgency
Degree to which the clinical situation requires prompt action.communicate clinical urgency
review outcome
Decision or findings following the appropriate assessment.explain the review outcome
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Risk depends on the actual examination and circumstances. Do not promise zero risk or claim that every X-ray is forbidden during pregnancy.

C Notice the language

Explain why before asking

A personal question can feel intrusive when asked without context or in front of a companion. Offer appropriate privacy without making assumptions about identity, relationships, or reproductive history.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Give the reason
This question is part of our examination-screening process.
Offer privacy
Would you prefer to discuss this without your companion present?
Allow uncertainty
It is all right to say that you are unsure.
Record accurately
I will record uncertainty rather than a negative answer.
Avoid a blanket rule
The appropriate team needs to review the actual examination.
Explain the referral
I will arrange the relevant clinical discussion.
Protect the boundary
I cannot give an individualized risk estimate from this information alone.
Return with a plan
We will explain the reviewed plan before proceeding.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Unsure versus no
Preserves unresolved information instead of forcing a binary answer.
Actual examination
Keeps the discussion specific rather than applying a rule to every imaging procedure.
Would you prefer
Offers a privacy choice without assuming who should be present.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which entry matches Rowan's response?

Answer and explanation

A. Unsure

Uncertainty is the supplied answer and must not be silently converted into a negative.

2. Which statement overpromises?

Answer and explanation

B. There is absolutely no risk in every case.

A universal zero-risk statement is not supported by the individualized assessment.

3. What should guide the next step?

Answer and explanation

B. The actual protocol and clinical review

The relevant examination and clinical circumstances require the appropriate professional process.

4. Which phrase supports privacy?

Answer and explanation

B. We can discuss this without your companion present.

Offering a private conversation respects the patient's stated preference and sensitive information.

E Conversations

A private answer before the examination

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Taylor: Radiographer / Rowan: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • benefit-risk discussion
  • radiologist review
  • private conversation
