    I need to ask a question required by our screening process. Would you prefer to discuss it privately?
  2. Rowan
    Yes. My companion does not know everything about my situation, and I would rather answer without them here.
  3. Taylor
    We can arrange a 1. I will explain why the question matters before asking for personal information.
  4. Rowan
    Thank you. I may not be able to give a definite answer about whether I could be pregnant.
  5. Taylor
    Please say that clearly. 2 is important information; it should not be changed to no.
  6. Rowan
    Does being unsure automatically mean that every kind of X-ray has to be canceled?
  7. Taylor
    No blanket conclusion follows. The 3 and clinical circumstances guide the appropriate review.
  8. Rowan
    I would like someone to explain the possible benefit and risk of this particular examination.
  9. Taylor
    I can arrange a 4 with the appropriate professional, without inventing an individual estimate myself.
  10. Rowan
    I have heard the word radiation, but I do not know what kind this examination uses.
  11. Taylor
    X-rays use 5. The team can explain what that means for the proposed examination and your circumstances.
  12. Rowan
    Will my uncertain answer still be visible when the person reviewing the examination reads the record?
  13. Taylor
    Yes. The 6 must reflect what you said, not a more convenient definite answer.
  14. Rowan
    Could the reason the examination is needed also matter to that discussion?
  15. Taylor
    Yes. 7 concerns whether the expected benefit warrants the examination in its clinical context.
  16. Rowan
    And I would like my companion included only after we agree what can be discussed with them.
  17. Taylor
    I will respect that 8 within the applicable rules and explain any relevant limits.
  18. Rowan
    Who will tell me what the review means for the next step?
  19. Taylor
    We will arrange the appropriate 9 or other authorized clinical review, then explain the outcome and next step.
  20. Rowan
    That gives me a clear process without pretending the 10 has already been decided.

Answers and explanations
  1. private conversation Turn 3. A private conversation respects the request to answer without the companion.
  2. Screening uncertainty Turn 5. Screening uncertainty must remain unresolved rather than being recorded as no.
  3. examination protocol Turn 7. The examination protocol governs this procedure, not every imaging situation universally.
  4. benefit-risk discussion Turn 9. A benefit-risk discussion weighs this examination's expected benefit and possible harm.
  5. ionizing radiation Turn 11. Ionizing radiation describes the radiation type, not a personal risk estimate.
  6. screening response Turn 13. The screening response records what the patient actually answered, including uncertainty, so the review is based on accurate information.
  7. Justification Turn 15. Justification considers whether the expected benefit warrants the examination in the actual clinical circumstances.
  8. privacy preference Turn 17. A privacy preference identifies the patient's wishes about who hears personal information and should be handled within the applicable rules.
  9. radiologist review Turn 19. Radiologist review is assessment by the imaging physician when required by the relevant process, not a decision supplied by this language exercise.
  10. review outcome Turn 20. The review outcome is the decision or findings after assessment and must not be presented as settled before that review occurs.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Taylor: I need to ask a question required by our screening process. Would you prefer to discuss it privately?
  2. Rowan: Yes. My companion does not know everything about my situation, and I would rather answer without them here.
  3. Taylor: We can arrange a private conversation. I will explain why the question matters before asking for personal information.
  4. Rowan: Thank you. I may not be able to give a definite answer about whether I could be pregnant.
  5. Taylor: Please say that clearly. Screening uncertainty is important information; it should not be changed to no.
  6. Rowan: Does being unsure automatically mean that every kind of X-ray has to be canceled?
  7. Taylor: No blanket conclusion follows. The examination protocol and clinical circumstances guide the appropriate review.
  8. Rowan: I would like someone to explain the possible benefit and risk of this particular examination.
  9. Taylor: I can arrange a benefit-risk discussion with the appropriate professional, without inventing an individual estimate myself.
  10. Rowan: I have heard the word radiation, but I do not know what kind this examination uses.
  11. Taylor: X-rays use ionizing radiation. The team can explain what that means for the proposed examination and your circumstances.
  12. Rowan: Will my uncertain answer still be visible when the person reviewing the examination reads the record?
  13. Taylor: Yes. The screening response must reflect what you said, not a more convenient definite answer.
  14. Rowan: Could the reason the examination is needed also matter to that discussion?
  15. Taylor: Yes. Justification concerns whether the expected benefit warrants the examination in its clinical context.
  16. Rowan: And I would like my companion included only after we agree what can be discussed with them.
  17. Taylor: I will respect that privacy preference within the applicable rules and explain any relevant limits.
  18. Rowan: Who will tell me what the review means for the next step?
  19. Taylor: We will arrange the appropriate radiologist review or other authorized clinical review, then explain the outcome and next step.
  20. Rowan: That gives me a clear process without pretending the review outcome has already been decided.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The answer needs privacy

A patient asks to answer screening questions alone.

  1. Patient: Could we discuss that screening question without my companion in the room?
  2. Radiographer: We can arrange appropriate privacy and explain why the question is being asked.
  3. Patient: My companion does not know the information, and I do not want them answering for me.
  4. Radiographer: Their presence does not automatically authorize them to provide or receive every detail.
  5. Patient: I may be unsure of the answer even when we speak privately.
  6. Radiographer: Please say that. Uncertainty should not be recorded as a negative response.
  7. Patient: Will an uncertain answer automatically cancel every possible imaging examination?
  8. Radiographer: No universal conclusion follows; the actual examination and clinical review determine the process.
  9. Patient: I would like the reviewed plan explained before anyone assumes I have agreed to proceed.
  10. Radiographer: We will keep the screening response, review outcome, and appropriate agreement distinct.
Unsure was entered as no

A radiographer corrects a screening record.

  1. Radiographer: The patient said unsure, but the screening field was recorded as no.
  2. Supervisor: That needs correction through the approved process, with the uncertainty preserved.
  3. Radiographer: I have not proceeded on the assumption that pregnancy has been excluded.
  4. Supervisor: Good. A binary field should not force the patient's actual answer into a false category.
  5. Radiographer: The original entry and correction need to remain traceable.
  6. Supervisor: Yes. Follow the record process and the appropriate clinical review for the examination.
  7. Radiographer: Should the team know that the answer changed because of an entry error?
  8. Supervisor: They need the accurate current information and relevant correction history.
  9. Radiographer: I will not describe the correction as a new confirmed pregnancy finding.
  10. Supervisor: Correct. Restoring uncertainty is different from establishing either a positive or negative conclusion.
A risk question needs the actual examination

A patient asks about a blanket radiation claim.

  1. Patient: Someone told me every X-ray has exactly the same risk during pregnancy. Is that accurate?
  2. Radiographer: The actual examination and circumstances matter; a blanket statement is not an individualized assessment.
  3. Patient: Can you give me a precise personal estimate from this short conversation?
  4. Radiographer: That requires the appropriate professional review, not an invented number.
  5. Patient: I would like the reason for the examination considered along with the possible harm.
  6. Radiographer: That is part of the relevant benefit-and-risk discussion with the authorized team.
  7. Patient: Does asking for that explanation mean I have already refused the examination?
  8. Radiographer: No. A request for information is not automatically a decision to decline.
  9. Patient: Please record my question and arrange the actual review rather than choosing an answer for me.
  10. Radiographer: We will preserve your concern and explain the verified next step through the department process.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A private answer before the examination
  1. Read the corrected exchange. Keep unsure distinct from no throughout.
  2. Swap roles. Explain privacy and review without supplying a risk estimate or examination decision.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A companion asks for the answer

A companion asks what Rowan said. Rowan has requested privacy and has not authorized sharing the answer. The clinical review remains pending.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Companion: "Can you share the screening ___?"

Answer and explanation

C. answer

The companion asks for the content of the screening response.

2. Radiographer: "Sharing has not been ___."

Answer and explanation

B. authorized

Rowan has not authorized disclosure of the private answer.

3. Companion: "The review is still ___?"

Answer and explanation

C. pending

The clinical review has not yet reached an outcome.

4. Radiographer: "I need to respect the requested ___."

Answer and explanation

B. privacy

The scenario explicitly requires respecting Rowan's request for privacy.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Original fictional case: Rowan is asked a pregnancy-screening question under the department protocol and requests privacy from a companion. Rowan is unsure of the answer. Communication goal: Explain a pregnancy-screening question respectfully and preserve an uncertain answer.
Language guidance: Unsure is not no. Screening information supports the appropriate review; it does not create a universal instruction to cancel or proceed.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This question is part of our examination-screening process. / Would you prefer to discuss this without your companion present? / It is all right to say that you are unsure. / I will record uncertainty rather than a negative answer. / The appropriate team needs to review the actual examination. / I will arrange the relevant clinical discussion. / I cannot give an individualized risk estimate from this information alone. / We will explain the reviewed plan before proceeding. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: pregnancy screening: Process of checking relevant pregnancy information before an examination. / possible pregnancy: Pregnancy that has not been confirmed or excluded. / screening uncertainty: Unresolved information from a screening question or process. / private conversation: Discussion held away from people not authorized to hear it. / sensitive history: Personal information requiring respectful and appropriate handling. / ionizing radiation: Radiation capable of removing electrons from atoms. / radiation exposure: Exposure to radiation during an examination or other event. / justification: Assessment that an examination's expected benefit warrants its use. / optimization: Adjusting imaging practice to achieve its purpose with appropriate exposure. / benefit-risk discussion: Comparison of expected benefit and relevant possible harm. / radiologist review: Assessment by a physician specializing in medical imaging. / examination protocol: Approved approach for a particular imaging examination. / screening response: Recorded answer to a screening question. / exposure history: Account of relevant prior radiation exposure. / reproductive history: Relevant history concerning pregnancy and reproduction. / privacy preference: Person's stated wishes about who hears personal information. / clinical urgency: Degree to which the clinical situation requires prompt action. / review outcome: Decision or findings following the appropriate assessment. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Helping a child cooperateA rehearsal before the picture+

Your goal

Use concrete language, bounded choices, and honest reassurance with a child.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

A rehearsal before the picture

Eight-year-old Mina is anxious about a requested chest X-ray. Radiographer Lee explains the equipment and rehearses the relevant instruction before acquisition. Mina worries that the machine will touch her. Her caregiver is available, but any presence or assistance during exposure depends on the department safety process. The exercise does not prescribe restraint or a caregiver position.

Lee: Radiographer / Mina: Child patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What worries Mina?

Answer and explanation

A. Whether the machine will touch her

Mina asks about the equipment and whether it will touch her.

2. What happens before acquisition?

Answer and explanation

C. Explanation and rehearsal

Lee explains the equipment and rehearses the instruction before acquisition.

3. What controls caregiver assistance?

Answer and explanation

D. The department safety process

Presence or assistance during exposure follows the actual safety process.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Assent and caregiver permission are not interchangeable. Requirements depend on age, capacity, law, and setting; involve the child appropriately without inventing a legal rule.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
age-appropriate explanation
Explanation adapted to the child's understanding and communication needs.give an age-appropriate explanation
assent
Child's affirmative agreement appropriate to age and circumstances.seek meaningful assent
caregiver permission
Authorization from an appropriate caregiver when required.verify caregiver permission
child-life support
Specialist support helping children cope with healthcare experiences.request child-life support
demonstration
Showing an action before asking someone to perform it.offer a demonstration
practice run
Rehearsal before the actual procedure or acquisition.complete a practice run
bounded choice
Limited genuine choice within the available safe options.offer a bounded choice
sensory description
Explanation of what someone may see, hear, or feel.provide a sensory description
distress cue
Sign suggesting anxiety, discomfort, or difficulty coping.notice a distress cue
coping strategy
Method helping a person manage an uncomfortable experience.agree on a coping strategy
caregiver presence
Caregiver being with the child during a relevant stage.clarify caregiver presence
protective arrangement
Appropriate safety setup for people involved in an examination.confirm protective arrangements
stillness
Absence of movement during the relevant acquisition interval.explain brief stillness
exposure signal
Cue associated with the actual imaging exposure.explain the exposure signal
reassurance
Supportive explanation that does not promise an unsupported outcome.give honest reassurance
stop signal
Agreed cue for communicating a need to pause.agree on a stop signal
cooperation
Participation supported by understanding and appropriate assistance.support cooperation
preparation sequence
Ordered explanation and steps before the examination.simplify the preparation sequence
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Avoid promises such as nothing can hurt or your caregiver can always stand here. Explain the actual equipment and approved arrangements honestly.

C Notice the language

Include the child directly

Speak to the child rather than only about the child. A choice about explanation or rehearsal is meaningful; do not offer a false choice about a decision that has not been assessed.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Address the child
Mina, I will explain what happens before we start.
Describe honestly
I can show you which part makes the picture.
Offer a real choice
Would you like me to explain first or demonstrate first?
Rehearse
We can practice that instruction before taking the picture.
Invite a question
Which part worries you most?
Bound the reassurance
I will tell you before we change position.
Explain caregiver arrangements
We need to check where your caregiver can safely be.
Agree on a pause
Let us agree how you can tell me you need a pause.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
First or first
Offers a choice about the order of explanation, not a false choice about clinical authorization.
Before we start
Gives the child a predictable sequence and time for questions.
Can safely be
Makes caregiver placement conditional on the actual safety arrangements.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which choice is genuine here?

Answer and explanation

B. Explanation first or demonstration first

The available choice concerns preparation order, not technical safety or diagnostic outcomes.

2. Which reassurance is honest?

Answer and explanation

B. I will explain before we change position.

Explaining the sequence is a commitment within the speaker's control rather than an unsupported guarantee.

3. What does a practice run do?

Answer and explanation

D. Rehearses an instruction before acquisition

Rehearsal supports understanding but does not settle consent, diagnosis, or every response.

4. Which terms require distinction?

Answer and explanation

C. Assent and caregiver permission

Child assent and legally required caregiver permission serve different roles in the actual setting.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

A rehearsal before the picture

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Lee: Radiographer / Mina: Child patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • assent
  • practice run
  • demonstration
  • exposure signal
  • age-appropriate explanation
  • stop signal
  • preparation sequence
  • protective arrangement
  • bounded choice
  • sensory description
  1. Lee
    Mina, I will explain the equipment before we begin. Which part of having the picture taken worries you most?
  2. Mina
    The big machine looks as though it might move toward me. I do not know what it does.
  3. Lee
    Let me give a 1 of this equipment, then show you the part used for the picture.
  4. Mina
    Could you show me first? It is easier when I can see what you mean.
  5. Lee
    Yes. That is a useful 2: demonstration first, then we can talk through the instructions.
  6. Mina
    Do I have to understand every long word before we can do the examination?
  7. Lee
    No. An 3 uses words that make sense to you. You can ask me to explain anything again.
  8. Mina
    I heard someone say stay still. I am worried that I will forget what to do.
  9. Lee
    We can have a 4 before the picture, so you know the sequence rather than guessing during it.
  10. Mina
    Will you tell me when the practice finishes and when the real picture begins?
  11. Lee
    Yes. I will explain the 5 used here and distinguish practice from the actual picture.
  12. Mina
    Can my caregiver stay beside me the whole time? I feel calmer when I can see them.
  13. Lee
    We need to check the 6 before promising where anyone can stand during exposure.
  14. Mina
    All right. Could you also tell me before asking me to move into a different position?
  15. Lee
    Certainly. A clear 7 helps you know what happens next. I will explain before changing the instruction.
  16. Mina
    What should I do if I get frightened or do not understand the next step?
  17. Lee
    Let us agree on a 8 so you can tell me. We will explain how to use it here.
  18. Mina
    I would like to practice with you before the picture. That feels less surprising.
  19. Lee
    Your 9 matters. We also follow the required permission and safety process with your caregiver.
  20. Mina
    I understand. The 10 first, then a practice, and I can tell you when I need something explained again.

Answers and explanations
  1. sensory description Turn 3. A sensory description explains what the child may see, hear, or feel rather than relying on technical labels alone.
  2. bounded choice Turn 5. A bounded choice offers a real limited option, here the order of explanation and demonstration, within the available safe process.
  3. age-appropriate explanation Turn 7. An age-appropriate explanation adapts the language to the child's understanding without withholding necessary information or speaking only to adults.
  4. practice run Turn 9. A practice run rehearses the instruction before the actual image is acquired so uncertainty can be addressed beforehand.
  5. exposure signal Turn 11. The exposure signal is the cue associated with the actual acquisition and should be distinguished from a rehearsal.
  6. protective arrangement Turn 13. A protective arrangement is the approved safety setup and determines where another person can appropriately be during the examination.
  7. preparation sequence Turn 15. A preparation sequence tells the child what comes next and reduces surprise without making promises about every sensation or outcome.
  8. stop signal Turn 17. A stop signal gives the child a clear way to communicate a need for a pause and should be explained within the actual procedure.
  9. assent Turn 19. Assent is the child's affirmative agreement appropriate to the circumstances and does not replace any legally required caregiver permission.
  10. demonstration Turn 20. A demonstration shows the action before the child is asked to perform it and makes the instruction more concrete.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Lee: Mina, I will explain the equipment before we begin. Which part of having the picture taken worries you most?
  2. Mina: The big machine looks as though it might move toward me. I do not know what it does.
  3. Lee: Let me give a sensory description of this equipment, then show you the part used for the picture.
  4. Mina: Could you show me first? It is easier when I can see what you mean.
  5. Lee: Yes. That is a useful bounded choice: demonstration first, then we can talk through the instructions.
  6. Mina: Do I have to understand every long word before we can do the examination?
  7. Lee: No. An age-appropriate explanation uses words that make sense to you. You can ask me to explain anything again.
  8. Mina: I heard someone say stay still. I am worried that I will forget what to do.
  9. Lee: We can have a practice run before the picture, so you know the sequence rather than guessing during it.
  10. Mina: Will you tell me when the practice finishes and when the real picture begins?
  11. Lee: Yes. I will explain the exposure signal used here and distinguish practice from the actual picture.
  12. Mina: Can my caregiver stay beside me the whole time? I feel calmer when I can see them.
  13. Lee: We need to check the protective arrangement before promising where anyone can stand during exposure.
  14. Mina: All right. Could you also tell me before asking me to move into a different position?
  15. Lee: Certainly. A clear preparation sequence helps you know what happens next. I will explain before changing the instruction.
  16. Mina: What should I do if I get frightened or do not understand the next step?
  17. Lee: Let us agree on a stop signal so you can tell me. We will explain how to use it here.
  18. Mina: I would like to practice with you before the picture. That feels less surprising.
  19. Lee: Your assent matters. We also follow the required permission and safety process with your caregiver.
  20. Mina: I understand. The demonstration first, then a practice, and I can tell you when I need something explained again.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A child chooses the preparation order

A radiographer offers a bounded choice.

  1. Radiographer: Would you like me to explain the picture first or show the equipment first?
  2. Child: Please show me first, because the machine looks different from what I expected.
  3. Radiographer: That is a choice we can make. I will explain each part as we go.
  4. Child: Does choosing the demonstration mean I have already agreed to every movement?
  5. Radiographer: No. We still explain the examination and follow the appropriate permission and safety process.
  6. Child: I want to know when something is practice and when the actual picture happens.
  7. Radiographer: I will make that distinction clear rather than ask you to guess.
  8. Child: Can I tell you when I do not understand a word?
  9. Radiographer: Yes. Questions help me explain the instruction in a way that makes sense.
  10. Child: Then I can choose the preparation order without being surprised by the next step.
The caregiver's place needs checking

A child asks where a caregiver can stand.

  1. Child: Can my caregiver stand right beside me for the whole examination?
  2. Radiographer: We need to check the actual safety arrangements before promising where anyone can stand.
  3. Child: I thought being allowed into the room meant every position was permitted.
  4. Radiographer: Entering the room and being present during exposure can involve different arrangements.
  5. Child: Could you explain where they will be before we start the actual picture?
  6. Radiographer: Yes. We should explain the approved plan so you know what to expect.
  7. Child: If I feel worried, I would like a clear way to tell you.
  8. Radiographer: We can agree on an appropriate pause signal and check that you understand it.
  9. Child: I do not need a promise that nothing can ever feel uncomfortable.
  10. Radiographer: Honest explanation and an agreed process are more useful than a guarantee we cannot make.
A long instruction is broken into steps

A child forgets part of a rehearsal.

  1. Child: I remembered the first movement, but I forgot the rest of the instruction.
  2. Radiographer: That tells me the explanation was too much at once. Let us separate the steps.
  3. Child: Are you going to call me difficult because I did not remember all three?
  4. Radiographer: No. We need an instruction you can understand, not a label about your behavior.
  5. Child: Can we practice one part before adding another?
  6. Radiographer: Yes. This is still preparation, and no image has been acquired.
  7. Child: Please tell me clearly when the practice is finished.
  8. Radiographer: I will explain the sequence and check your understanding before the actual examination.
  9. Child: That gives me time to ask instead of guessing what comes next.
  10. Radiographer: Exactly. A shorter instruction can support cooperation without pressure or surprise.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A rehearsal before the picture
  1. Read the corrected exchange, using a warm but precise voice for the instructions.
  2. Swap roles. Practice the real preparation choice without promising a result or caregiver position.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

The instruction was too long

Noah forgets a three-part instruction during practice. The radiographer breaks it into one step at a time. No image has yet been acquired.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Noah: "I forgot the three-part ___."

Answer and explanation

A. instruction

Noah reports difficulty remembering the instruction during the practice.

2. Radiographer: "We can use one ___ at a time."

Answer and explanation

D. step

The supplied adaptation is a single step at a time.

3. Noah: "This is still ___."

Answer and explanation

C. practice

The scenario states that acquisition has not yet begun.

4. Radiographer: "No image has been ___."

Answer and explanation

C. taken

The rehearsal occurs before the actual image is acquired.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 04: Helping a child cooperate
Original fictional case: Eight-year-old Mina is anxious about a requested chest X-ray. Radiographer Lee explains the equipment and rehearses the relevant instruction before acquisition. Mina worries that the machine will touch her. Her caregiver is available, but any presence or assistance during exposure depends on the department safety process. The exercise does not prescribe restraint or a caregiver position.
Language workshop: A rehearsal before the picture
Communication goal: Use concrete language, bounded choices, and honest reassurance with a child.
Language guidance: Assent and caregiver permission are not interchangeable. Requirements depend on age, capacity, law, and setting; involve the child appropriately without inventing a legal rule.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Mina, I will explain what happens before we start. / I can show you which part makes the picture. / Would you like me to explain first or demonstrate first? / We can practice that instruction before taking the picture. / Which part worries you most? / I will tell you before we change position. / We need to check where your caregiver can safely be. / Let us agree how you can tell me you need a pause. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: age-appropriate explanation: Explanation adapted to the child's understanding and communication needs. / assent: Child's affirmative agreement appropriate to age and circumstances. / caregiver permission: Authorization from an appropriate caregiver when required. / child-life support: Specialist support helping children cope with healthcare experiences. / demonstration: Showing an action before asking someone to perform it. / practice run: Rehearsal before the actual procedure or acquisition. / bounded choice: Limited genuine choice within the available safe options. / sensory description: Explanation of what someone may see, hear, or feel. / distress cue: Sign suggesting anxiety, discomfort, or difficulty coping. / coping strategy: Method helping a person manage an uncomfortable experience. / caregiver presence: Caregiver being with the child during a relevant stage. / protective arrangement: Appropriate safety setup for people involved in an examination. / stillness: Absence of movement during the relevant acquisition interval. / exposure signal: Cue associated with the actual imaging exposure. / reassurance: Supportive explanation that does not promise an unsupported outcome. / stop signal: Agreed cue for communicating a need to pause. / cooperation: Participation supported by understanding and appropriate assistance. / preparation sequence: Ordered explanation and steps before the examination. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Coordinating portable imagingThe bedside team stays connected+

Your goal

Coordinate equipment, patient movement, and responsibilities before a bedside examination.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The bedside team stays connected

Radiographer Sam arrives for a portable chest examination. Nurse Priya identifies attached lines and monitoring equipment. Sam asks for the appropriate bedside team to coordinate any movement or equipment management. No line is disconnected, no device setting is changed, and no examination is performed until the actual preparation and safety checks are complete.

Sam: Radiographer / Priya: Bedside nurse

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What examination is requested?

Answer and explanation

D. A portable chest examination

The brief specifies a portable chest examination at the bedside.

2. Who identifies attached equipment?

Answer and explanation

B. Priya

Priya is the bedside nurse identifying lines and monitoring equipment.

3. What has not happened?

Answer and explanation

B. A line disconnection

The case explicitly states that no line has been disconnected.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Recognizing an attached line does not authorize disconnecting or adjusting it. Identify the responsible professional and coordinate the actual preparation.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
portable radiography
Radiographic imaging performed with mobile equipment at the patient's location.coordinate portable radiography
bedside examination
Examination performed where the patient is receiving bedside care.prepare a bedside examination
mobile X-ray unit
Movable equipment used to acquire radiographs outside a fixed room.position the mobile X-ray unit
detector placement
Positioning the image receptor for the intended examination.coordinate detector placement
attached line
Tube or connection linked to the patient's care.identify attached lines
monitoring equipment
Devices used to observe relevant physiological information.protect monitoring equipment
oxygen delivery device
Equipment providing prescribed oxygen support.identify the oxygen delivery device
bedside coordination
Joint planning of activity around the patient's immediate care.request bedside coordination
equipment clearance
Available space for positioning and moving equipment.check equipment clearance
infection precaution
Measure intended to reduce transmission of infection.follow infection precautions
isolation status
Applicable infection-control designation for the patient or area.verify isolation status
cleaning protocol
Approved process for decontaminating equipment or surfaces.follow the cleaning protocol
patient support
Assistance needed to maintain a suitable and safe position.arrange patient support
line management
Authorized handling and protection of clinical tubing or connections.coordinate line management
team readiness
Confirmation that required people and preparations are in place.confirm team readiness
equipment adjustment
Change to a device's position or settings within authorized practice.request authorized equipment adjustment
handoff acknowledgment
Confirmation that a transfer of information has been received.obtain handoff acknowledgment
bedside constraint
Limitation affecting an examination at the patient's location.communicate bedside constraints
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Portable does not mean informal. Identity, examination, infection-control, movement, and exposure checks still follow the department process.

C Notice the language

State the dependency directly

A polite request can still be explicit: identify the preparation needed and who is authorized to provide it. Do not treat clinical equipment as furniture.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the request
I am here for the requested portable chest examination.
Ask about constraints
What attached equipment affects positioning?
Name the dependency
I need the appropriate team to coordinate movement first.
Protect connections
I will not disconnect that line or change its settings.
Clarify responsibility
Who is authorized to manage this device?
Check preparation
Please confirm when the bedside preparation is complete.
State a limitation
The available space limits where the unit can be positioned.
Close the handoff
Please acknowledge the preparation plan before we proceed.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Need before
Makes preparation a prerequisite rather than an optional courtesy.
Manage versus move
Clinical equipment may require authorized management, not merely physical relocation.
Please confirm when
Requests a clear readiness signal instead of assuming silence means agreement.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which request identifies a dependency?

Answer and explanation

B. I need authorized line management before movement.

The statement names the required preparation and does not assume authority to perform it.

2. What does portable describe?

Answer and explanation

A. Equipment and examination location

Portable describes how imaging reaches the patient, not a waiver of safeguards.

3. Which action is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. Disconnecting an attached line independently

No independent disconnection is authorized in the supplied case.

4. What confirms readiness?

Answer and explanation

C. Explicit acknowledgment from the relevant team

Readiness requires the actual preparations and team acknowledgment, not a single convenient cue.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

The bedside team stays connected

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sam: Radiographer / Priya: Bedside nurse

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • team readiness
  • isolation status
  • bedside coordination
  • handoff acknowledgment
  • oxygen delivery device
  • patient support
  • equipment clearance
  • line management
  • cleaning protocol
  • detector placement
  1. Sam
    I am here for the requested portable chest examination. Before positioning, can we review the bedside constraints together?
  2. Priya
    Yes. There are attached lines and monitoring devices, and movement needs coordination with the appropriate bedside staff.
  3. Sam
    This 1 is important. I will not treat the connections as objects I can simply move aside.
  4. Priya
    Thank you. I can identify them and confirm who is authorized to manage each device.
  5. Sam
    Please include the 2 in that review. I will not disconnect it or change any setting.
  6. Priya
    Understood. We also need to confirm the precautions currently applying to this patient and room.
  7. Sam
    I will check the 3 and follow the required preparation for the equipment and examination.
  8. Priya
    Space is limited on the far side of the bed. Does that affect where your unit can go?
  9. Sam
    Yes. We need sufficient 4 without interfering with care or assuming that a device can be relocated.
  10. Priya
    I will ask the appropriate colleague to help coordinate the movement and protect the connections.
  11. Sam
    That will support 5. We should agree on the sequence before placing anything or moving the patient.
  12. Priya
    The patient also needs support to maintain the position. We have not yet assessed the exact assistance.
  13. Sam
    Then 6 remains an outstanding preparation. I will not describe the bedside as ready yet.
  14. Priya
    I will confirm the assistance and line responsibilities through our actual clinical process.
  15. Sam
    Please name who will handle 7 so that nobody assumes someone else has accepted that responsibility.
  16. Priya
    Agreed. I will give you an explicit update when the required people and preparations are in place.
  17. Sam
    That confirms 8, rather than treating an unanswered request as permission to begin.
  18. Priya
    Afterward, the equipment also needs the appropriate cleaning before it leaves the area.
  19. Sam
    Yes. I will follow the 9 and communicate any unresolved issue through the department process.
  20. Priya
    We have a clear 10: preparation first, explicit readiness, then the examination under the actual safety process.

Answers and explanations
  1. bedside coordination Turn 3. Bedside coordination prepares movement and equipment management with the relevant staff.
  2. oxygen delivery device Turn 5. An oxygen delivery device requires appropriately authorized handling; it is not furniture.
  3. isolation status Turn 7. Isolation status identifies the actual infection-control requirements, not an assumed precaution.
  4. equipment clearance Turn 9. Equipment clearance means the space required for appropriate positioning and movement.
  5. detector placement Turn 11. Detector placement may require coordinated patient support and protection of attached equipment.
  6. patient support Turn 13. Patient support is the assistance required for the position and must be assessed rather than assumed from the imaging request.
  7. line management Turn 15. Line management involves authorized protection and handling of clinical tubing or connections rather than independent adjustment by anyone present.
  8. team readiness Turn 17. Team readiness means the required staff and preparations are actually in place, not merely that the radiographer has arrived.
  9. cleaning protocol Turn 19. The cleaning protocol is the approved decontamination process appropriate to the equipment and infection-control requirements.
  10. handoff acknowledgment Turn 20. Handoff acknowledgment confirms receipt of the preparation plan and responsibilities instead of leaving them as an unconfirmed message.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sam: I am here for the requested portable chest examination. Before positioning, can we review the bedside constraints together?
  2. Priya: Yes. There are attached lines and monitoring devices, and movement needs coordination with the appropriate bedside staff.
  3. Sam: This bedside coordination is important. I will not treat the connections as objects I can simply move aside.
  4. Priya: Thank you. I can identify them and confirm who is authorized to manage each device.
  5. Sam: Please include the oxygen delivery device in that review. I will not disconnect it or change any setting.
  6. Priya: Understood. We also need to confirm the precautions currently applying to this patient and room.
  7. Sam: I will check the isolation status and follow the required preparation for the equipment and examination.
  8. Priya: Space is limited on the far side of the bed. Does that affect where your unit can go?
  9. Sam: Yes. We need sufficient equipment clearance without interfering with care or assuming that a device can be relocated.
  10. Priya: I will ask the appropriate colleague to help coordinate the movement and protect the connections.
  11. Sam: That will support detector placement. We should agree on the sequence before placing anything or moving the patient.
  12. Priya: The patient also needs support to maintain the position. We have not yet assessed the exact assistance.
  13. Sam: Then patient support remains an outstanding preparation. I will not describe the bedside as ready yet.
  14. Priya: I will confirm the assistance and line responsibilities through our actual clinical process.
  15. Sam: Please name who will handle line management so that nobody assumes someone else has accepted that responsibility.
  16. Priya: Agreed. I will give you an explicit update when the required people and preparations are in place.
  17. Sam: That confirms team readiness, rather than treating an unanswered request as permission to begin.
  18. Priya: Afterward, the equipment also needs the appropriate cleaning before it leaves the area.
  19. Sam: Yes. I will follow the cleaning protocol and communicate any unresolved issue through the department process.
  20. Priya: We have a clear handoff acknowledgment: preparation first, explicit readiness, then the examination under the actual safety process.
Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A line is not something to move aside

A radiographer coordinates bedside equipment.

  1. Radiographer: The attached line affects detector placement. Who is authorized to manage it during preparation?
  2. Nurse: I will identify the appropriate colleague rather than assume you should disconnect it.
  3. Radiographer: Thank you. Recognizing the obstacle does not give me authority to alter the connection.
  4. Nurse: Correct. The patient's actual equipment and care needs must guide the preparation.
  5. Radiographer: We should agree on the movement sequence before anyone places the detector.
  6. Nurse: Yes, with the relevant support and line responsibilities explicit.
  7. Radiographer: I will not treat silence as confirmation that the bedside is ready.
  8. Nurse: I will give an actual readiness update once the required arrangements are in place.
  9. Radiographer: Then the examination can proceed without an informal assumption about equipment management.
  10. Nurse: That is the purpose of the coordination: clear roles alongside the real clinical checks.
Isolation information needs verification

Two staff members clarify bedside precautions.

  1. Radiographer: Before bringing the mobile equipment closer, I need to verify the current precautions for this room.
  2. Nurse: I will confirm the actual isolation information rather than rely on an older note.
  3. Radiographer: The absence of a sign should not become proof that no precaution applies.
  4. Nurse: Agreed. We need the current service information and the approved process.
  5. Radiographer: I also need the equipment-cleaning arrangements that apply after this examination.
  6. Nurse: Those should match the actual equipment and infection-control requirements.
  7. Radiographer: Could we distinguish what has been verified from what someone merely remembers?
  8. Nurse: Yes. The preparation record should not turn an uncertain recollection into a current instruction.
  9. Radiographer: I will explain any pause to the patient without disclosing unrelated information.
  10. Nurse: Then the examination can be coordinated with both clear communication and the appropriate precautions.
Readiness is not the same as arrival

A lead technologist checks a portable examination status.

  1. Lead technologist: You have arrived at the bedside. Are the required preparations actually complete?
  2. Radiographer: Not yet. Patient support has been requested, but the assisting colleague has not arrived.
  3. Lead technologist: Then the status should remain awaiting preparation, not ready for acquisition.
  4. Radiographer: I will keep that distinction clear and coordinate with the bedside team.
  5. Lead technologist: Is the request for assistance enough to assume someone has accepted responsibility?
  6. Radiographer: No. We need the actual acknowledgment and the required people in place.
  7. Lead technologist: Please also identify any equipment-clearance issue before moving the mobile unit.
  8. Radiographer: I have noted the available space and will not relocate clinical equipment independently.
  9. Lead technologist: Once the actual checks are complete, confirm the readiness through the agreed process.
  10. Radiographer: I will. Arrival, a request, and confirmed readiness are separate stages.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The bedside team stays connected
  1. Read the corrected bedside exchange, emphasizing the preparation dependencies.
  2. Swap roles. Name the responsible team without assigning yourself an unauthorized equipment task.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Readiness is not yet confirmed

The nurse has requested transfer assistance, but the assisting colleague has not arrived. The radiographer waits for the actual readiness confirmation.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Nurse: "Transfer assistance has been ___."

Answer and explanation

B. requested

A request has been made but is not proof that assistance is present.

2. Radiographer: "The colleague has not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

D. arrived

The scenario explicitly says the assisting colleague has not arrived.

3. Nurse: "Readiness is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

A. confirmed

The required assistance remains outstanding, so readiness is not established.

4. Radiographer: "I will wait for the actual ___."

Answer and explanation

B. acknowledgment

Explicit confirmation is required before treating the preparation as complete.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Explaining a technical limitationA repeat is a reviewed decision+

Your goal

Describe image quality precisely and distinguish technical review from diagnosis.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

A repeat is a reviewed decision

Radiographer Ari identifies motion affecting a chest image and asks senior radiographer Devon to review technical adequacy. The case does not supply a diagnostic interpretation or authorize an automatic repeat. The team will assess whether the image answers the clinical question and whether any additional acquisition is justified under the actual process.

Ari: Radiographer / Devon: Senior radiographer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What limitation is identified?

Answer and explanation

A. Motion affecting the image

The supplied technical concern is motion affecting the chest image.

2. Who reviews technical adequacy?

Answer and explanation

B. Devon

Devon is the senior radiographer reviewing the technical concern.

3. What is not authorized automatically?

Answer and explanation

A. A repeat exposure

The case requires assessment rather than an automatic additional acquisition.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A technical limitation is not a diagnosis. Exposure index is not a direct patient-dose reading, and a displayed image should not be judged from brightness alone.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
motion artifact
Image distortion caused by movement during acquisition.identify motion artifact
technical adequacy
Fitness of an image's technical features for its intended purpose.review technical adequacy
diagnostic adequacy
Whether available images sufficiently address the clinical question.assess diagnostic adequacy
repeat exposure
Additional radiation exposure to acquire another image.justify a repeat exposure
collimation
Restriction of the X-ray beam to the intended area.assess collimation
exposure index
System indicator related to detector exposure under specified conditions.interpret the exposure index
detector exposure
Radiation reaching the image receptor during acquisition.review detector exposure
image noise
Unwanted variation affecting the visual signal in an image.assess image noise
image contrast
Difference in appearance between image regions or structures.assess image contrast
spatial resolution
Ability to distinguish nearby structures in an image.describe spatial resolution
anatomical coverage
Extent of the required anatomy included in an image.check anatomical coverage
positioning error
Departure from the intended patient or anatomical position.describe a positioning error
exposure factor
Technical parameter affecting an imaging exposure.review exposure factors
image processing
Computational treatment of acquired image data.distinguish image processing
reject analysis
Review of rejected images to identify patterns and improvement opportunities.conduct reject analysis
quality assurance
Organized activities checking and improving process quality.support quality assurance
technical review
Assessment of relevant acquisition and image-quality features.request a technical review
additional acquisition
Further collection of image data after an initial acquisition.assess additional acquisition
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Retain images and follow the actual review and repeat process. Do not hide a rejected image or automatically repeat solely because a picture looks imperfect.

C Notice the language

Describe the limitation, not the blame

Use observable technical language. A patient may move because of pain, breathlessness, anxiety, or an unclear instruction; do not label the person difficult.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Describe the issue
There is visible motion affecting this image.
Request review
Could you review whether the image answers the clinical question?
Avoid blame
I will document the limitation without labeling the patient.
Avoid automatic repetition
A repeat needs the appropriate assessment and justification.
Separate measures
The exposure index is not a direct patient-dose reading.
Preserve the record
I will retain the image within the approved record.
Clarify the purpose
We need adequate information, not merely a prettier picture.
Support improvement
Let us identify what can improve the next instruction.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Affecting versus invalidating
A limitation may affect an image without automatically making the entire examination unusable.
Whether
Frames adequacy as a question for review rather than a conclusion already reached.
Additional versus replacement
An additional exposure has consequences; it is not merely an edit to an existing picture.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement is precise?

Answer and explanation

C. Motion affects this image and needs review.

Describing the image's motion limitation identifies the review need without blame or an unsupported diagnosis.

2. What is not a direct patient-dose reading?

Answer and explanation

B. Exposure index

Exposure index concerns detector exposure under specified conditions rather than directly reporting individual patient dose.

3. Why review before repeating?

Answer and explanation

A. To assess adequacy and justify any additional acquisition

Review connects the additional exposure decision to clinical purpose and appropriate justification.

4. Which practice preserves integrity?

Answer and explanation

A. Retaining the image through the approved process

Preserving the image and approved record supports review and accurate quality improvement.

E Conversations

A repeat is a reviewed decision

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ari: Radiographer / Devon: Senior radiographer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • technical adequacy
  • technical review
  • Quality assurance
  • Reject analysis
  • diagnostic adequacy
  • additional acquisition
  • motion artifact
  • anatomical coverage
  • repeat exposure
  • exposure index
  1. Ari
    This chest image has visible motion. Could you review its technical adequacy before we decide whether anything further is needed?
  2. Devon
    Yes. Describe the limitation specifically, and keep the original image available in the approved record.
  3. Ari
    The 1 affects part of the image. I have not concluded that the whole examination is unusable.
  4. Devon
    Good. We need to assess the actual clinical question, not simply aim for a more attractive picture.
  5. Ari
    So 2 is different from making every image look technically perfect.
  6. Devon
    Exactly. The relevant professional review determines what information is sufficient and what limitation matters.
  7. Ari
    I also checked the 3, rather than relying only on the overall brightness of the displayed image.
  8. Devon
    That is useful. Display appearance and processing do not independently establish appropriate exposure or adequacy.
  9. Ari
    The system shows an 4. I should not describe that as a direct reading of the patient's dose.
  10. Devon
    Correct. Interpret the indicator according to the actual system and context, without substituting it for another quantity.
  11. Ari
    Does identifying the motion authorize me to acquire another image immediately?
  12. Devon
    No. A 5 needs appropriate assessment and justification under our process.
  13. Ari
    I will also avoid recording the patient as difficult. We have not established why the movement occurred.
  14. Devon
    Yes. 6 should examine the process, including whether our instructions were clear and manageable.
  15. Ari
    If another acquisition is authorized, the instruction may need to be explained or rehearsed differently.
  16. Devon
    That belongs in the 7, alongside the actual clinical and technical constraints.
  17. Ari
    The original image will remain available even if the process classifies it as unsuitable.
  18. Devon
    Correct. 8 depends on an accurate record, not deleting evidence of a problem.
  19. Ari
    I will document the limitation and the review outcome before treating an 9 as agreed.
  20. Devon
    That keeps 10, diagnostic interpretation, and the decision about another exposure distinct.

Answers and explanations
  1. motion artifact Turn 3. Motion artifact describes movement-related distortion, without assigning blame or a diagnosis.
  2. diagnostic adequacy Turn 5. Diagnostic adequacy concerns the clinical question, not a visually perfect picture.
  3. anatomical coverage Turn 7. Anatomical coverage identifies whether the required body area is included.
  4. exposure index Turn 9. Exposure index concerns detector exposure, not a direct patient-dose measurement.
  5. repeat exposure Turn 12. A repeat exposure needs appropriate review and justification, not automatic repetition.
  6. Quality assurance Turn 14. Quality assurance checks and improves the process and should use accurate observations rather than unsupported judgments about the patient.
  7. technical review Turn 16. Technical review assesses acquisition and image-quality features and can identify improvements without turning them into a diagnosis.
  8. Reject analysis Turn 18. Reject analysis reviews rejected images for improvement and depends on an accurate retained record rather than concealed evidence.
  9. additional acquisition Turn 19. An additional acquisition collects further image data and must be based on the appropriate reviewed decision rather than assumed permission.
  10. technical adequacy Turn 20. Technical adequacy concerns the image's technical fitness for purpose and must be distinguished from a diagnostic interpretation.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ari: This chest image has visible motion. Could you review its technical adequacy before we decide whether anything further is needed?
  2. Devon: Yes. Describe the limitation specifically, and keep the original image available in the approved record.
  3. Ari: The motion artifact affects part of the image. I have not concluded that the whole examination is unusable.
  4. Devon: Good. We need to assess the actual clinical question, not simply aim for a more attractive picture.
  5. Ari: So diagnostic adequacy is different from making every image look technically perfect.
  6. Devon: Exactly. The relevant professional review determines what information is sufficient and what limitation matters.
  7. Ari: I also checked the anatomical coverage, rather than relying only on the overall brightness of the displayed image.
  8. Devon: That is useful. Display appearance and processing do not independently establish appropriate exposure or adequacy.
  9. Ari: The system shows an exposure index. I should not describe that as a direct reading of the patient's dose.
  10. Devon: Correct. Interpret the indicator according to the actual system and context, without substituting it for another quantity.
  11. Ari: Does identifying the motion authorize me to acquire another image immediately?
  12. Devon: No. A repeat exposure needs appropriate assessment and justification under our process.
  13. Ari: I will also avoid recording the patient as difficult. We have not established why the movement occurred.
  14. Devon: Yes. Quality assurance should examine the process, including whether our instructions were clear and manageable.
  15. Ari: If another acquisition is authorized, the instruction may need to be explained or rehearsed differently.
  16. Devon: That belongs in the technical review, alongside the actual clinical and technical constraints.
  17. Ari: The original image will remain available even if the process classifies it as unsuitable.
  18. Devon: Correct. Reject analysis depends on an accurate record, not deleting evidence of a problem.
  19. Ari: I will document the limitation and the review outcome before treating an additional acquisition as agreed.
  20. Devon: That keeps technical adequacy, diagnostic interpretation, and the decision about another exposure distinct.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Motion is a description, not blame

A radiographer discusses image quality with a senior colleague.

  1. Radiographer: There is motion affecting the image, but I do not know why the patient moved.
  2. Senior colleague: Then describe the artifact rather than label the patient uncooperative.
  3. Radiographer: The instruction may have been difficult to follow, although that has not been established.
  4. Senior colleague: We can review the process without turning a possible explanation into a proven cause.
  5. Radiographer: Does identifying motion automatically mean the image must be repeated?
  6. Senior colleague: No. The appropriate review considers whether the clinical question is adequately addressed.
  7. Radiographer: I will preserve the original image and request the actual technical assessment.
  8. Senior colleague: Good. An imperfect appearance is not by itself authorization for another exposure.
  9. Radiographer: The record should separate the limitation, review outcome, and any authorized next acquisition.
  10. Senior colleague: Exactly. That supports image integrity and useful quality improvement.
An exposure indicator is misunderstood

A trainee asks about a system value.

  1. Trainee: The system displays an exposure index. Can I call it the patient's radiation dose?
  2. Senior radiographer: No. It is a detector-related indicator under the system's specified conditions, not a direct patient-dose reading.
  3. Trainee: Then its meaning requires the actual equipment information and acquisition context.
  4. Senior radiographer: Correct. We should not rename one quantity as another because both involve exposure.
  5. Trainee: Does the displayed image brightness alone tell us whether the exposure was appropriate?
  6. Senior radiographer: No. Processing and display can affect appearance, so that shortcut is unreliable.
  7. Trainee: I should review the relevant technical information through the approved process.
  8. Senior radiographer: Yes, and distinguish the technical assessment from a diagnostic interpretation.
  9. Trainee: Could we explain the limitation without giving a dose estimate that was never measured?
  10. Senior radiographer: That is essential. Precise terminology prevents a number from implying more than it establishes.
A rejected image remains part of review

Two staff members discuss record integrity.

  1. Radiographer: The image may be rejected after review. Should it disappear from the record?
  2. Quality lead: Follow the approved retention process; do not hide evidence of the acquisition.
  3. Radiographer: I thought deleting it would make the examination look cleaner.
  4. Quality lead: A cleaner-looking record is not the goal if it removes information needed for review.
  5. Radiographer: Reject analysis depends on an accurate account of what was acquired and why it was unsuitable.
  6. Quality lead: Exactly. We need the actual limitation and decision, not a reconstructed perfect history.
  7. Radiographer: Should the review focus on a particular person before the circumstances are established?
  8. Quality lead: No. Start with verified observations and examine the process rather than assume blame.
  9. Radiographer: I will keep the image and relevant information available through the authorized system.
  10. Quality lead: That supports a traceable record and a meaningful opportunity to improve the work.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A repeat is a reviewed decision
  1. Read the corrected exchange. Distinguish the limitation from the decision about repeating.
  2. Swap roles. Explain exposure index without giving a dose estimate or selecting exposure settings.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A missing area needs review

A required anatomical area is not fully included. The image is preserved. The appropriate reviewer has not yet decided whether additional acquisition is needed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Radiographer: "The anatomical coverage is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. incomplete

The required area is not fully included in the supplied image.

2. Reviewer: "The image must remain ___."

Answer and explanation

D. available

The scenario explicitly preserves the image for appropriate review.

3. Radiographer: "Additional acquisition is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

B. decided

The reviewer has not yet made the additional-acquisition decision.

4. Reviewer: "We need the appropriate ___."

Answer and explanation

A. assessment

The decision requires the actual assessment rather than an automatic repeat.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.
