TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.

Lesson 06: Explaining a technical limitation
Original fictional case: Radiographer Ari identifies motion affecting a chest image and asks senior radiographer Devon to review technical adequacy. The case does not supply a diagnostic interpretation or authorize an automatic repeat. The team will assess whether the image answers the clinical question and whether any additional acquisition is justified under the actual process.
Language workshop: A repeat is a reviewed decision
Communication goal: Describe image quality precisely and distinguish technical review from diagnosis.
Language guidance: A technical limitation is not a diagnosis. Exposure index is not a direct patient-dose reading, and a displayed image should not be judged from brightness alone.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): There is visible motion affecting this image. / Could you review whether the image answers the clinical question? / I will document the limitation without labeling the patient. / A repeat needs the appropriate assessment and justification. / The exposure index is not a direct patient-dose reading. / I will retain the image within the approved record. / We need adequate information, not merely a prettier picture. / Let us identify what can improve the next instruction. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: motion artifact: Image distortion caused by movement during acquisition. / technical adequacy: Fitness of an image's technical features for its intended purpose. / diagnostic adequacy: Whether available images sufficiently address the clinical question. / repeat exposure: Additional radiation exposure to acquire another image. / collimation: Restriction of the X-ray beam to the intended area. / exposure index: System indicator related to detector exposure under specified conditions. / detector exposure: Radiation reaching the image receptor during acquisition. / image noise: Unwanted variation affecting the visual signal in an image. / image contrast: Difference in appearance between image regions or structures. / spatial resolution: Ability to distinguish nearby structures in an image. / anatomical coverage: Extent of the required anatomy included in an image. / positioning error: Departure from the intended patient or anatomical position. / exposure factor: Technical parameter affecting an imaging exposure. / image processing: Computational treatment of acquired image data. / reject analysis: Review of rejected images to identify patterns and improvement opportunities. / quality assurance: Organized activities checking and improving process quality. / technical review: Assessment of relevant acquisition and image-quality features. / additional acquisition: Further collection of image data after an initial acquisition. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected exchange. Distinguish the limitation from the decision about repeating. Swap roles. Explain exposure index without giving a dose estimate or selecting exposure settings. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Ari: Radiographer / Devon: Senior radiographer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A required anatomical area is not fully included. The image is preserved. The appropriate reviewer has not yet decided whether additional acquisition is needed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ari: The original image will remain available even if the process classifies it as unsuitable.
Devon: Correct. Reject analysis depends on an accurate record, not deleting evidence of a problem.
Ari: I will document the limitation and the review outcome before treating an additional acquisition as agreed.
Devon: That keeps technical adequacy, diagnostic interpretation, and the decision about another exposure distinct.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Optional second-round challenge: A required anatomical area is not fully included. The image is preserved. The appropriate reviewer has not yet decided whether additional acquisition is needed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ari: The original image will remain available even if the process classifies it as unsuitable.
Devon: Correct. Reject analysis depends on an accurate record, not deleting evidence of a problem.
Ari: I will document the limitation and the review outcome before treating an additional acquisition as agreed.
Devon: That keeps technical adequacy, diagnostic interpretation, and the decision about another exposure distinct.
END REFERENCE
07Responding to requests for resultsExplain the process without interpreting the image+

Your goal

Set a helpful professional boundary and give a concrete route for results.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Explain the process without interpreting the image

After an examination, patient Jules asks radiographer Ren whether a visible mark means cancer. Ren has not issued a diagnostic interpretation. The reporting clinician will interpret the images, and the referring service will explain the result through its actual process. No reporting deadline is supplied. Ren checks how Jules can contact the service rather than promising a result time.

Ren: Radiographer / Jules: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Jules ask about?

Answer and explanation

D. Whether a mark means cancer

Jules asks an anxious question but no diagnostic interpretation is supplied.

2. Who interprets the images?

Answer and explanation

D. The reporting clinician

The reporting clinician is responsible for the diagnostic interpretation in this case.

3. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. A reporting deadline

No deadline is given, so Ren must not invent a result time.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A visible feature is not an established diagnosis. Acquiring the image does not automatically authorize diagnostic interpretation.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
image interpretation
Professional assessment of images for clinical meaning.arrange image interpretation
reporting clinician
Authorized professional responsible for the imaging interpretation.identify the reporting clinician
radiology report
Recorded interpretation of an imaging examination.review the radiology report
preliminary report
Initial interpretation that may be updated after further review.distinguish a preliminary report
final report
Completed authorized report under the relevant reporting process.communicate the final report
incidental finding
Finding outside the main reason for the examination.explain an incidental finding
comparison images
Earlier images used to assess relevant differences.obtain comparison images
referring service
Clinical service that requested or coordinates the examination.contact the referring service
result communication
Process of explaining a result to the appropriate recipient.confirm result communication
report turnaround
Time between the relevant examination stage and report availability.verify report turnaround
critical result
Finding requiring urgent communication through the relevant clinical pathway.escalate a critical result
report addendum
Additional or corrected information appended to a report.review a report addendum
clinical correlation
Interpretation of a finding alongside other clinical information.explain clinical correlation
patient portal
Secure system allowing a patient to access relevant health information.explain patient-portal access
result inquiry
Request for information about an examination result.handle a result inquiry
scope of practice
Duties a professional is trained and authorized to perform.respect scope of practice
follow-up contact
Named person or service for subsequent communication.provide a follow-up contact
unverified impression
Initial belief not established by the appropriate interpretation.avoid an unverified impression
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Report availability and explanation to the patient are separate steps. Do not promise a deadline or assume portal release completes clinical communication.

C Notice the language

A boundary should include a next step

A bare I cannot say may feel dismissive. Acknowledge the concern, explain your role, and identify the person or service responsible for interpretation and communication.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the concern
I can hear that the mark is worrying you.
Clarify your role
My role here is to acquire the images.
Name the interpreter
The reporting clinician will interpret the examination.
Avoid speculation
I cannot identify that as a diagnosis from this conversation.
Give a route
Let us confirm how the referring service will explain the result.
Limit timing
I need to verify the reporting arrangements rather than guess.
Separate access and explanation
Seeing a report online is not the same as having it explained.
Offer a concrete contact
I can help identify the appropriate contact for your question.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Will interpret versus has confirmed
Distinguishes a pending professional process from a completed finding.
Cannot identify as
Sets a precise boundary without dismissing the person's concern.
Verify rather than guess
Avoids turning an uncertain administrative timeframe into a promise.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response is most helpful?

Answer and explanation

D. I cannot interpret that, but I can confirm the reporting route.

The response combines an accurate professional boundary with a concrete next step.

2. Which distinction matters?

Answer and explanation

B. Report access and clinical explanation

A patient may access a report before its meaning has been explained in context.

3. What timing claim is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

C. The result will definitely be ready tomorrow.

The case provides no reporting deadline, so tomorrow would be an invented promise.

4. What does clinical correlation mean?

Answer and explanation

B. Interpreting a finding with other clinical information

Clinical correlation places the image finding within the wider clinical evidence.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Explain the process without interpreting the image

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ren: Radiographer / Jules: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • patient portal
  • result inquiry
  • reporting clinician
  • Comparison images
  • follow-up contact
  • unverified impression
  • Clinical correlation
  • scope of practice
  • Result communication
  • report turnaround
  1. Jules
    I saw a mark on the screen. Does that mean cancer, or can you tell me it is harmless?
  2. Ren
    I can hear that it worries you. My role here is to acquire images, not give their diagnostic interpretation.
  3. Jules
    But you see these pictures every day. Surely you have an idea of what that mark means.
  4. Ren
    An 1 would not give you a reliable answer. I do not want to mislead you in either direction.
  5. Jules
    Then who can answer the question properly, and how will the information get back to me?
  6. Ren
    The 2 interprets the examination, and we should confirm how your referring service communicates the result.
  7. Jules
    Is a report the same thing as someone discussing what it means for my situation?
  8. Ren
    No. 3 includes explaining it in context, not merely creating a document in the system.
  9. Jules
    I sometimes see reports on my phone before anyone has spoken to me about them.
  10. Ren
    A 4 can provide access, but seeing the words is not the same as discussing their meaning with the appropriate clinician.
  11. Jules
    If it says correlate clinically, does that mean the picture did not work?
  12. Ren
    5 means considering the finding with other clinical information. It is not automatically a statement that acquisition failed.
  13. Jules
    Could an earlier examination help the person who is preparing the report?
  14. Ren
    6 may provide useful context. The team follows its process for obtaining and reviewing them.
  15. Jules
    Can you promise that someone will call tomorrow? Waiting without a contact is the hardest part.
  16. Ren
    I need to verify the 7 arrangements rather than guess. No definite reporting time has been established in this conversation.
  17. Jules
    I understand the limit, but I would still appreciate knowing whom to contact with the question.
  18. Ren
    Let us identify the 8 and the referring service's route for discussing your result.
  19. Jules
    That is more useful than a guess. I can ask about the report through that route.
  20. Ren
    Yes. Respecting 9 still allows a helpful handoff for your 10.

Answers and explanations
  1. unverified impression Turn 4. An unverified impression is not a reliable diagnosis or reassurance.
  2. reporting clinician Turn 6. The reporting clinician interprets images; acquiring them is a different responsibility.
  3. Result communication Turn 8. Result communication includes explanation, not merely a report existing in the system.
  4. patient portal Turn 10. Patient-portal access does not establish that the report has been clinically explained.
  5. Clinical correlation Turn 12. Clinical correlation interprets imaging alongside the patient's other relevant clinical information.
  6. Comparison images Turn 14. Comparison images are earlier examinations used to assess differences and may provide context for the reporting clinician.
  7. report turnaround Turn 16. Report turnaround is the actual time to report availability and cannot be invented as a guaranteed deadline in this case.
  8. follow-up contact Turn 18. A follow-up contact gives the patient a concrete person or service for subsequent communication rather than a vague instruction to wait.
  9. scope of practice Turn 20. Scope of practice defines the duties a professional is authorized and competent to perform, including the boundary around diagnostic interpretation.
  10. result inquiry Turn 20. A result inquiry is a request about examination findings and deserves an appropriate communication route rather than unsupported speculation.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jules: I saw a mark on the screen. Does that mean cancer, or can you tell me it is harmless?
  2. Ren: I can hear that it worries you. My role here is to acquire images, not give their diagnostic interpretation.
  3. Jules: But you see these pictures every day. Surely you have an idea of what that mark means.
  4. Ren: An unverified impression would not give you a reliable answer. I do not want to mislead you in either direction.
  5. Jules: Then who can answer the question properly, and how will the information get back to me?
  6. Ren: The reporting clinician interprets the examination, and we should confirm how your referring service communicates the result.
  7. Jules: Is a report the same thing as someone discussing what it means for my situation?
  8. Ren: No. Result communication includes explaining it in context, not merely creating a document in the system.
  9. Jules: I sometimes see reports on my phone before anyone has spoken to me about them.
  10. Ren: A patient portal can provide access, but seeing the words is not the same as discussing their meaning with the appropriate clinician.
  11. Jules: If it says correlate clinically, does that mean the picture did not work?
  12. Ren: Clinical correlation means considering the finding with other clinical information. It is not automatically a statement that acquisition failed.
  13. Jules: Could an earlier examination help the person who is preparing the report?
  14. Ren: Comparison images may provide useful context. The team follows its process for obtaining and reviewing them.
  15. Jules: Can you promise that someone will call tomorrow? Waiting without a contact is the hardest part.
  16. Ren: I need to verify the report turnaround arrangements rather than guess. No definite reporting time has been established in this conversation.
  17. Jules: I understand the limit, but I would still appreciate knowing whom to contact with the question.
  18. Ren: Let us identify the follow-up contact and the referring service's route for discussing your result.
  19. Jules: That is more useful than a guess. I can ask about the report through that route.
  20. Ren: Yes. Respecting scope of practice still allows a helpful handoff for your result inquiry.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A worried patient asks for a diagnosis

A radiographer provides a helpful reporting handoff.

  1. Patient: You looked at the screen for a while. Does that mean you saw something serious?
  2. Radiographer: My work with the images does not establish a diagnosis I can give you in this conversation.
  3. Patient: I understand the boundary, but I do not want to leave without knowing the next step.
  4. Radiographer: Let us confirm the reporting process and who will explain the result.
  5. Patient: Can you promise that the result will be available tomorrow?
  6. Radiographer: I need to verify the actual arrangements rather than invent a deadline.
  7. Patient: I would appreciate a contact for questions about the result status.
  8. Radiographer: We can identify the appropriate referring service or reporting contact under our process.
  9. Patient: That is more helpful than either guessing a diagnosis or simply saying nothing.
  10. Radiographer: Exactly. A clear boundary should still include a useful route for your question.
The report is online but unexplained

A patient asks what portal access means.

  1. Patient: I can see a report in the portal, but nobody has explained it to me.
  2. Radiographer: Access to a report and a clinical discussion of its meaning are different steps.
  3. Patient: Could you interpret the unfamiliar terms from the image while I am here?
  4. Radiographer: The appropriate reporting or referring clinician needs to explain the actual findings in context.
  5. Patient: I do not want to assume that every highlighted phrase is a confirmed diagnosis.
  6. Radiographer: That is a reason to use the proper clinical explanation rather than guess from the wording.
  7. Patient: Who should I contact if the report arrived before my follow-up discussion?
  8. Radiographer: Let us confirm the actual contact route through the service.
  9. Patient: Then portal release should not be recorded as though the clinical explanation has already happened.
  10. Radiographer: Correct. The communication status should describe what actually occurred.
A preliminary report is not the final version

A coordinator checks which report is being discussed.

  1. Coordinator: The patient refers to a preliminary report, but a later version may be available.
  2. Radiographer: We need to verify the actual report status rather than assume the first copy is final.
  3. Coordinator: Should I describe the later document as a correction before checking its content?
  4. Radiographer: No. A later version and a confirmed correction are not automatically the same thing.
  5. Coordinator: The responsible clinician should explain any relevant change in the actual interpretation.
  6. Radiographer: Yes. We should not interpret the clinical significance from the document timestamp alone.
  7. Coordinator: I will identify the current authorized version and the appropriate contact for explanation.
  8. Radiographer: Please preserve the distinction between record availability and the patient's understanding.
  9. Coordinator: Then the patient can ask about the correct report rather than compare unexplained fragments.
  10. Radiographer: That supports a clear handoff without giving a diagnosis outside the relevant role.

  2. Swap roles. Respond to the request for certainty without supplying a diagnosis or a deadline.

The exercise provides access information but no clinical follow-up decision.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

REFERENCE
Course: X-Ray and Radiography English
Professional audience: X-ray technicians, radiologic technologists, radiographers, imaging department staff

Lesson 07: Responding to requests for results
Original fictional case: After an examination, patient Jules asks radiographer Ren whether a visible mark means cancer. Ren has not issued a diagnostic interpretation. The reporting clinician will interpret the images, and the referring service will explain the result through its actual process. No reporting deadline is supplied. Ren checks how Jules can contact the service rather than promising a result time.
Language workshop: Explain the process without interpreting the image
Communication goal: Set a helpful professional boundary and give a concrete route for results.
Language guidance: A visible feature is not an established diagnosis. Acquiring the image does not automatically authorize diagnostic interpretation.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I can hear that the mark is worrying you. / My role here is to acquire the images. / The reporting clinician will interpret the examination. / I cannot identify that as a diagnosis from this conversation. / Let us confirm how the referring service will explain the result. / I need to verify the reporting arrangements rather than guess. / Seeing a report online is not the same as having it explained. / I can help identify the appropriate contact for your question. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: image interpretation: Professional assessment of images for clinical meaning. / reporting clinician: Authorized professional responsible for the imaging interpretation. / radiology report: Recorded interpretation of an imaging examination. / preliminary report: Initial interpretation that may be updated after further review. / final report: Completed authorized report under the relevant reporting process. / incidental finding: Finding outside the main reason for the examination. / comparison images: Earlier images used to assess relevant differences. / referring service: Clinical service that requested or coordinates the examination. / result communication: Process of explaining a result to the appropriate recipient. / report turnaround: Time between the relevant examination stage and report availability. / critical result: Finding requiring urgent communication through the relevant clinical pathway. / report addendum: Additional or corrected information appended to a report. / clinical correlation: Interpretation of a finding alongside other clinical information. / patient portal: Secure system allowing a patient to access relevant health information. / result inquiry: Request for information about an examination result. / scope of practice: Duties a professional is trained and authorized to perform. / follow-up contact: Named person or service for subsequent communication. / unverified impression: Initial belief not established by the appropriate interpretation. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected result discussion, pairing every boundary with a useful next step. Swap roles. Respond to the request for certainty without supplying a diagnosis or a deadline. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Ren: Radiographer / Jules: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Pat can see a report online but has not discussed it with the referring clinician. No interpretation or follow-up decision is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jules: I understand the limit, but I would still appreciate knowing whom to contact with the question.
Ren: Let us identify the follow-up contact and the referring service's route for discussing your result.
Jules: That is more useful than a guess. I can ask about the report through that route.
Ren: Yes. Respecting scope of practice still allows a helpful handoff for your result inquiry.
END REFERENCE
08Reporting a labeling near missCorrect the record without hiding the history+

Your goal

Describe a discrepancy factually and request an authorized correction with an audit trail.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Correct the record without hiding the history

Radiographer Kim notices that an image label may not match the verified examination details. Kim stops further release and contacts supervisor Noor through the department process. The original record is preserved. The team has not established how the mismatch arose or whether any incorrect information reached another service. This is a communication case, not an instruction for editing clinical images.

Kim: Radiographer / Noor: Supervisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Kim notice?

Answer and explanation

B. A possible label mismatch

The case supplies a possible mismatch without establishing cause or downstream impact.

2. What happens to the original record?

Answer and explanation

C. It is preserved

Preserving the original record supports the authorized review and correction process.

3. What remains unestablished?

Answer and explanation

A. How the mismatch arose

The cause and any downstream impact have not yet been established.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A possible mismatch is not an established root cause. A near miss can be confirmed only when the relevant facts support that classification.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
label discrepancy
Conflict between an image label and verified examination information.report a label discrepancy
near miss
Event caught before the relevant potential harm occurs.report a potential near miss
incident report
Formal record of an event through the applicable reporting system.submit an incident report
audit trail
Traceable history of actions and changes in a record.preserve the audit trail
record integrity
Accuracy and trustworthiness of a maintained record.protect record integrity
data correction
Authorized amendment of inaccurate recorded information.request a data correction
release hold
Temporary prevention of further distribution pending review.apply an authorized release hold
downstream recipient
Person or system receiving information later in the workflow.identify downstream recipients
root cause
Underlying factor established through appropriate investigation.investigate the root cause
contributing factor
Circumstance that may have influenced an event.identify contributing factors
event chronology
Ordered account of what happened and when.document the event chronology
verified detail
Information checked against an appropriate source.distinguish verified details
unconfirmed impact
Possible consequence not yet established by review.state unconfirmed impact
notification route
Approved path for informing the relevant people.follow the notification route
correction authorization
Approval required to amend a relevant record.obtain correction authorization
preserved original
Original information retained through the approved process.maintain the preserved original
system reconciliation
Process of aligning related records across relevant systems.coordinate system reconciliation
learning review
Structured review intended to improve future practice.support a learning review
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Follow the real incident, correction, and notification processes. Never conceal evidence, overwrite history informally, or assume that fixing one display corrects every linked system.

C Notice the language

A correction should remain traceable

Report what is known promptly, identify what remains unverified, and preserve the record. Do not turn a suspected mismatch into a proven cause or quietly erase its history.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the discrepancy
The label may not match the verified examination details.
State the containment
Further release is paused through our process.
Preserve uncertainty
The cause and downstream impact are not yet established.
Keep the original
I have preserved the original record.
Request authorization
Please confirm the approved correction route.
Separate action and investigation
The immediate containment does not settle the root cause.
Check linked records
We need to verify which systems and recipients are affected.
Close factually
I will record the confirmed actions and outstanding questions.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
May not match
Describes a suspected discrepancy without pretending the investigation is complete.
Not yet established
Preserves uncertainty about cause and impact without minimizing the event.
Corrected versus concealed
A traceable authorized amendment differs from an invisible alteration.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is factual?

Answer and explanation

B. The label may not match verified details.

The wording reports the observed concern without inventing cause or impact.

2. What must remain traceable?

Answer and explanation

A. The original record and authorized changes

An audit trail preserves the history needed to understand and verify a correction.

3. Which conclusion is premature?

Answer and explanation

C. No downstream recipient was affected.

The case explicitly leaves downstream impact unverified, so absence of impact cannot be asserted.

4. What is system reconciliation for?

Answer and explanation

C. Aligning related records across affected systems

Reconciliation checks related records so a local correction is not mistaken for complete resolution.

Correct the record without hiding the history

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Kim: Radiographer / Noor: Supervisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • release hold
  • correction authorization
  • label discrepancy

  • audit trail
  • root cause

  • event chronology
  • preserved original
  • record integrity
  1. Kim
    I have noticed a possible mismatch between the image label and the verified examination details. Further release is paused.
  2. Noor
    Thank you for escalating it. Preserve the original record and state exactly what you have verified so far.
  3. Kim
    The 1 is the concern. I have not established how it arose or whether another service received it.
  4. Noor
    Good. Keep the event description separate from any assumption about who or what caused it.
  5. Kim
    I have applied the authorized 2 through our process, rather than making an undocumented change to the image.
  6. Noor
    We need the approved correction route and an account of what happened in order.
  7. Kim
    I will give an 3 using verified times and actions, marking anything I have not confirmed.
  8. Noor
    Include the point at which you noticed the issue and the people contacted afterward.
  9. Kim
    The 4 remains available. I have not deleted it or substituted an earlier image.
  10. Noor
    That protects the record while the appropriate people determine the correction and notification requirements.
  11. Kim
    Should I call this a near miss now, or wait until the impact is known?
  12. Noor
    Use the appropriate classification after review. 5 is not the same as proof that no one received incorrect information.
  13. Kim
    We need to identify every relevant 6 through the authorized investigation rather than assume the local screen is the only record.
  14. Noor
    Correct. Fixing one display does not prove that all related records are aligned.
  15. Kim
    I will request 7 and keep the actions visible in the approved system.
  16. Noor
    We also need the 8, including what was changed, by whom, and through which process.
  17. Kim
    Once the immediate issue is contained, the investigation can examine contributing factors without guessing at blame.
  18. Noor
    Yes. Establishing a 9 requires investigation; it is not settled by the first report.
  19. Kim
    I will complete the required report and flag the unresolved system and recipient questions.
  20. Noor
    That supports 10 and a useful learning review while the actual correction process continues.

Answers and explanations
  1. label discrepancy Turn 3. A label discrepancy is a conflict between the image label and verified examination information and should be described before its cause is assumed.
  2. release hold Turn 5. A release hold temporarily prevents further distribution while the relevant concern is reviewed through the approved process.
  3. event chronology Turn 7. An event chronology records the sequence and timing of known actions, which helps review without turning guesses into facts.
  4. preserved original Turn 9. A preserved original retains the initial information through the approved process so the review and any correction remain traceable.
  5. Unconfirmed impact Turn 12. Unconfirmed impact means possible consequences have not yet been established and cannot be described as definitely absent.
  6. downstream recipient Turn 13. A downstream recipient is a later person or system receiving the information and may require appropriate notification or correction.
  7. correction authorization Turn 15. Correction authorization is the required approval for amending the relevant record rather than an informal independent alteration.
  8. audit trail Turn 16. An audit trail preserves the history of actions and changes so a correction can be understood and verified later.
  9. root cause Turn 18. A root cause is an underlying factor supported by investigation, not an assumption made from the first visible discrepancy.
  10. record integrity Turn 20. Record integrity is the accuracy and trustworthiness of the maintained record, including traceable corrections and unresolved facts.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Kim: I have noticed a possible mismatch between the image label and the verified examination details. Further release is paused.
  2. Noor: Thank you for escalating it. Preserve the original record and state exactly what you have verified so far.
  3. Kim: The label discrepancy is the concern. I have not established how it arose or whether another service received it.
  4. Noor: Good. Keep the event description separate from any assumption about who or what caused it.
  5. Kim: I have applied the authorized release hold through our process, rather than making an undocumented change to the image.
  6. Noor: We need the approved correction route and an account of what happened in order.
  7. Kim: I will give an event chronology using verified times and actions, marking anything I have not confirmed.
  8. Noor: Include the point at which you noticed the issue and the people contacted afterward.
  9. Kim: The preserved original remains available. I have not deleted it or substituted an earlier image.
  10. Noor: That protects the record while the appropriate people determine the correction and notification requirements.
  11. Kim: Should I call this a near miss now, or wait until the impact is known?
  12. Noor: Use the appropriate classification after review. Unconfirmed impact is not the same as proof that no one received incorrect information.
  13. Kim: We need to identify every relevant downstream recipient through the authorized investigation rather than assume the local screen is the only record.
  14. Noor: Correct. Fixing one display does not prove that all related records are aligned.
  15. Kim: I will request correction authorization and keep the actions visible in the approved system.
  16. Noor: We also need the audit trail, including what was changed, by whom, and through which process.
  17. Kim: Once the immediate issue is contained, the investigation can examine contributing factors without guessing at blame.
  18. Noor: Yes. Establishing a root cause requires investigation; it is not settled by the first report.
  19. Kim: I will complete the required report and flag the unresolved system and recipient questions.
  20. Noor: That supports record integrity and a useful learning review while the actual correction process continues.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A suspected mismatch is not a proven cause

A radiographer reports a labeling concern.

  1. Radiographer: The image label may not match the verified examination details. I have paused further release.
  2. Supervisor: Thank you. Preserve the record and state exactly what is known.
  3. Radiographer: I have not established how the mismatch arose or whether another service received the information.
  4. Supervisor: Keep those questions open rather than state that no impact occurred.
  5. Radiographer: Should I identify a person as responsible because their name appears in the workflow?
  6. Supervisor: No. The cause requires the appropriate investigation, not an assumption from one visible entry.
  7. Radiographer: I will document the event sequence and the actual containment action.
  8. Supervisor: Please use verified times and distinguish them from estimates or later recollections.
  9. Radiographer: The correction will follow the authorized process with its history retained.
  10. Supervisor: That protects the record while the actual review determines the next steps.
One corrected screen is not every system

A supervisor checks the reach of a correction.

  1. Supervisor: The local record has been corrected through the approved process. Have linked systems been checked?
  2. Radiographer: Not yet. I cannot confirm that every downstream record now matches.
  3. Supervisor: Then full reconciliation remains incomplete, even though the local display looks correct.
  4. Radiographer: I will identify the relevant systems and recipients through the authorized review process.
  5. Supervisor: We should not assume that a local edit automatically updates every earlier message.
  6. Radiographer: Agreed. The actual distribution and correction arrangements need verification.
  7. Supervisor: Please retain the original and the authorized change history.
  8. Radiographer: They remain available through the approved record system for traceability.
  9. Supervisor: I will keep the outstanding reconciliation tasks visible with named responsibility.
  10. Radiographer: That prevents a partial correction from being reported as complete resolution.
A near-miss label needs evidence

Two staff members discuss incident classification.

  1. Radiographer: Can I call this a near miss before we know whether the incorrect information reached anyone?
  2. Safety lead: The classification should follow the actual facts and reporting process.
  3. Radiographer: I wanted to reassure the team that the problem was caught.
  4. Safety lead: You can state the containment action without claiming that no relevant harm or exposure occurred.
  5. Radiographer: The downstream impact is still being reviewed, so I should say unconfirmed.
  6. Safety lead: Yes. Unconfirmed impact is not the same as established absence of impact.
  7. Radiographer: I will report the verified chronology and the questions that remain unresolved.
  8. Safety lead: That gives the review useful information without minimizing or exaggerating the event.
  9. Radiographer: Once the facts are established, the appropriate classification can be recorded.
  10. Safety lead: Exactly. Accurate reporting supports learning better than choosing the most reassuring label first.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Correct the record without hiding the history
  1. Read the corrected incident exchange, separating verified actions from unresolved cause and impact.
  2. Swap roles. Practice requesting a traceable correction without specifying an unauthorized editing method.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

One linked system is still unchecked

An authorized correction is complete in the local record. A linked system has not yet been checked. The original and change history are retained.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Reporter: "The local correction is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. complete

The supplied facts confirm completion only in the local record.

2. Supervisor: "The linked system remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unchecked

The scenario explicitly leaves the linked system unverified.

3. Reporter: "The original and history are ___."

Answer and explanation

B. retained

The record and change history remain available for traceability.

4. Supervisor: "Full reconciliation is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

B. confirmed

One unchecked linked system prevents claiming that reconciliation is complete.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.
Lesson 08: Reporting a labeling near miss
Original fictional case: Radiographer Kim notices that an image label may not match the verified examination details. Kim stops further release and contacts supervisor Noor through the department process. The original record is preserved. The team has not established how the mismatch arose or whether any incorrect information reached another service. This is a communication case, not an instruction for editing clinical images.
Language workshop: Correct the record without hiding the history
Communication goal: Describe a discrepancy factually and request an authorized correction with an audit trail.
Language guidance: A possible mismatch is not an established root cause. A near miss can be confirmed only when the relevant facts support that classification.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The label may not match the verified examination details. / Further release is paused through our process. / The cause and downstream impact are not yet established. / I have preserved the original record. / Please confirm the approved correction route. / The immediate containment does not settle the root cause. / We need to verify which systems and recipients are affected. / I will record the confirmed actions and outstanding questions. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: label discrepancy: Conflict between an image label and verified examination information. / near miss: Event caught before the relevant potential harm occurs. / incident report: Formal record of an event through the applicable reporting system. / audit trail: Traceable history of actions and changes in a record. / record integrity: Accuracy and trustworthiness of a maintained record. / data correction: Authorized amendment of inaccurate recorded information. / release hold: Temporary prevention of further distribution pending review. / downstream recipient: Person or system receiving information later in the workflow. / root cause: Underlying factor established through appropriate investigation. / contributing factor: Circumstance that may have influenced an event. / event chronology: Ordered account of what happened and when. / verified detail: Information checked against an appropriate source. / unconfirmed impact: Possible consequence not yet established by review. / notification route: Approved path for informing the relevant people. / correction authorization: Approval required to amend a relevant record. / preserved original: Original information retained through the approved process. / system reconciliation: Process of aligning related records across relevant systems. / learning review: Structured review intended to improve future practice. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected incident exchange, separating verified actions from unresolved cause and impact. Swap roles. Practice requesting a traceable correction without specifying an unauthorized editing method. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Kim: Radiographer / Noor: Supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An authorized correction is complete in the local record. A linked system has not yet been checked. The original and change history are retained.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Kim: Once the immediate issue is contained, the investigation can examine contributing factors without guessing at blame.
Noor: Yes. Establishing a root cause requires investigation; it is not settled by the first report.
Kim: I will complete the required report and flag the unresolved system and recipient questions.
Noor: That supports record integrity and a useful learning review while the actual correction process continues.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: X-Ray and Radiography English

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

Positioning instructions through an interpreter

Coordinate interpreted movement instructions without relying on a companion to guess the meaning.

Radiographer Ari and qualified interpreter Sol prepare an examination for patient Hana. The script shows coordination before speaking with Hana. The actual examination and patient ability determine the instructions. No positioning technique or permission to proceed is supplied by this language case.

Ari: Radiographer / Sol: Qualified interpreter

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • single-step instruction
  • informed agreement
  • understanding check
  • role boundaries
  • pause signal
  • patient response
  1. Ari
    I want Hana to understand each instruction before moving. Let us agree on short segments and a clear pause.
  2. Sol
    Please speak directly to Hana and let me interpret the complete instruction before adding another movement.
  3. Ari
    I will use a 1 rather than combine turning, lifting, and breathing into one long sentence.
  4. Sol
    That will help. If a term is unclear, I will request clarification rather than choose a movement by guesswork.
  5. Ari
    I also need to check what Hana can comfortably do before describing any position as agreed.
  6. Sol
    I can interpret the question and response without replacing the patient's account with my own assessment.
  7. Ari
    We should preserve the 2 exactly, especially if an instruction causes pain or confusion.
  8. Sol
    Yes. An interpreter's fluent answer must not be mistaken for the patient's agreement or ability to move.
  9. Ari
    Hana's companion has offered to show the movement, but the actual ability and safe approach still need assessment.
  10. Sol
    Qualified interpretation supports communication; it does not authorize a companion to move the patient or make clinical decisions.
  11. Ari
    I will ask Hana to explain the instruction back before any actual acquisition, when appropriate.
  12. Sol
    That 3 should be directed to Hana, not to me as though I were the patient.
  13. Ari
    If the instruction needs revision, I will say so clearly rather than change it halfway through your interpretation.
  14. Sol
    Please do. A transparent correction prevents the patient from receiving two incompatible instructions without knowing which is current.
  15. Ari
    We also need an agreed way for Hana to request a pause during the preparation.
  16. Sol
    A 4 can be explained and checked before the procedure begins.
  17. Ari
    I will not assume that a nod means every safety question has been answered.
  18. Sol
    Nor should silence be treated as 5 without the actual required process.
  19. Ari
    Once the explanation is clear, we will proceed only under the real examination checks and authorized plan.
  20. Sol
    That preserves 6 while making the instructions accessible to Hana.

Answers and explanations
  1. single-step instruction Turn 3. A single-step instruction asks for one action at a time and allows the patient to understand and respond before another action is added.
  2. patient response Turn 7. The patient response communicates the person's understanding, ability, or concern and must be interpreted rather than supplied by the interpreter.
  3. understanding check Turn 12. An understanding check asks the patient to demonstrate or restate the meaning and verifies the explanation rather than the interpreter's knowledge.
  4. pause signal Turn 16. A pause signal provides a clear way for the patient to communicate a need to stop or clarify within the actual examination process.
  5. informed agreement Turn 18. Informed agreement requires the relevant understanding and authorization rather than being inferred solely from silence or a nod during interpretation.
  6. role boundaries Turn 20. Role boundaries keep clinical assessment and authorization with the appropriate professionals while the interpreter supports accurate communication.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ari: I want Hana to understand each instruction before moving. Let us agree on short segments and a clear pause.
  2. Sol: Please speak directly to Hana and let me interpret the complete instruction before adding another movement.
  3. Ari: I will use a single-step instruction rather than combine turning, lifting, and breathing into one long sentence.
  4. Sol: That will help. If a term is unclear, I will request clarification rather than choose a movement by guesswork.
  5. Ari: I also need to check what Hana can comfortably do before describing any position as agreed.
  6. Sol: I can interpret the question and response without replacing the patient's account with my own assessment.
  7. Ari: We should preserve the patient response exactly, especially if an instruction causes pain or confusion.
  8. Sol: Yes. An interpreter's fluent answer must not be mistaken for the patient's agreement or ability to move.
  9. Ari: Hana's companion has offered to show the movement, but the actual ability and safe approach still need assessment.
  10. Sol: Qualified interpretation supports communication; it does not authorize a companion to move the patient or make clinical decisions.
  11. Ari: I will ask Hana to explain the instruction back before any actual acquisition, when appropriate.
  12. Sol: That understanding check should be directed to Hana, not to me as though I were the patient.
  13. Ari: If the instruction needs revision, I will say so clearly rather than change it halfway through your interpretation.
  14. Sol: Please do. A transparent correction prevents the patient from receiving two incompatible instructions without knowing which is current.
  15. Ari: We also need an agreed way for Hana to request a pause during the preparation.
  16. Sol: A pause signal can be explained and checked before the procedure begins.
  17. Ari: I will not assume that a nod means every safety question has been answered.
  18. Sol: Nor should silence be treated as informed agreement without the actual required process.
  19. Ari: Once the explanation is clear, we will proceed only under the real examination checks and authorized plan.
  20. Sol: That preserves role boundaries while making the instructions accessible to Hana.
Two movements were combined

The radiographer gives two movement instructions together. The interpreter requests separate instructions before the patient moves. The patient has not yet agreed to the movement.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Interpreter: "Please separate the two ___."

Answer and explanation

B. instructions

The interpreter asks for the combined movement directions to be given separately.

2. Radiographer: "I will give one ___ at a time."

Answer and explanation

D. movement

Each instruction will now address one movement rather than two together.

3. Interpreter: "The patient has not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

C. agreed

The scenario states that agreement to the movement is not yet established.

4. Radiographer: "We will check understanding before ___."

Answer and explanation

B. proceeding

The preparation requires a clear explanation and understanding before proceeding.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.

The imaging worklist is unavailable

Use the approved downtime process without creating duplicate or guessed patient records.

The imaging worklist is unavailable. Radiographer Ren contacts lead technologist Dev. They use the approved downtime process for identity, request verification, acquisition records, and later reconciliation. No substitute identifiers, device operation instructions, or authorization to bypass checks are supplied.

Ren: Radiographer / Dev: Lead technologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • reconciliation ownership
  • system outage
  • Duplicate prevention
  • patient matching
  • acquisition record
  • downtime procedure
  1. Ren
    The worklist is unavailable. I can see an appointment message, but I do not want to treat it as a verified order.
  2. Dev
    Use the approved downtime process. The outage does not remove identity or examination-verification requirements.
  3. Ren
    We need to confirm whether this is a 1 affecting the worklist rather than assume every system is unavailable.
  4. Dev
    Correct. State what is affected and what remains accessible without guessing about the technical cause.
  5. Ren
    I will not create a new record from a remembered name or use another patient's examination as a placeholder.
  6. Dev
    That protects 2 and avoids introducing a second identity problem while addressing the first.
  7. Ren
    What should we use for recording the examination if the normal electronic workflow cannot be completed?
  8. Dev
    Follow the actual 3, including the authorized documentation and verification steps for this service.
  9. Ren
    We also need a clear record of what was acquired and when, so the information can be reconciled later.
  10. Dev
    Yes. Preserve the required 4 under the approved process rather than relying on memory after the system returns.
  11. Ren
    Could entering the same request again create a duplicate if the original reappears?
  12. Dev
    It could create a discrepancy. Do not improvise another order or identifier outside the authorized process.
  13. Ren
    I will tell the team which examinations remain incomplete in the electronic workflow.
  14. Dev
    A clear status prevents an unfinished electronic step from being mistaken for an examination that never occurred.
  15. Ren
    When the system is restored, someone needs to check that the records match rather than assume the outage fixed itself.
  16. Dev
    Assign 5 and follow the actual review process.
  17. Ren
    That review should also check for duplicate entries and any information still missing.
  18. Dev
    Exactly. 6 is part of maintaining a trustworthy record.
  19. Ren
    I will report the affected workflow, use the approved process, and keep unresolved items visible.
  20. Dev
    Good. Restoration of system access is not proof that every examination record has been correctly reconciled.

Answers and explanations
  1. system outage Turn 3. A system outage is loss of access or function in the relevant system and requires the approved continuity process rather than informal workarounds.
  2. patient matching Turn 6. Patient matching links the correct person with the correct request and record and must remain reliable during downtime.
  3. downtime procedure Turn 8. The downtime procedure is the approved process for continuing appropriate work during a system interruption and must not be invented in a language exercise.
  4. acquisition record Turn 10. An acquisition record documents the actual examination data and timing and is needed for accurate later reconciliation.
  5. reconciliation ownership Turn 16. Reconciliation ownership identifies who will verify and align the downtime records with the restored systems instead of leaving the task unassigned.
  6. Duplicate prevention Turn 18. Duplicate prevention avoids creating multiple conflicting records for the same examination and remains important both during downtime and after restoration.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ren: The worklist is unavailable. I can see an appointment message, but I do not want to treat it as a verified order.
  2. Dev: Use the approved downtime process. The outage does not remove identity or examination-verification requirements.
  3. Ren: We need to confirm whether this is a system outage affecting the worklist rather than assume every system is unavailable.
  4. Dev: Correct. State what is affected and what remains accessible without guessing about the technical cause.
  5. Ren: I will not create a new record from a remembered name or use another patient's examination as a placeholder.
  6. Dev: That protects patient matching and avoids introducing a second identity problem while addressing the first.
  7. Ren: What should we use for recording the examination if the normal electronic workflow cannot be completed?
  8. Dev: Follow the actual downtime procedure, including the authorized documentation and verification steps for this service.
  9. Ren: We also need a clear record of what was acquired and when, so the information can be reconciled later.
  10. Dev: Yes. Preserve the required acquisition record under the approved process rather than relying on memory after the system returns.
  11. Ren: Could entering the same request again create a duplicate if the original reappears?
  12. Dev: It could create a discrepancy. Do not improvise another order or identifier outside the authorized process.
  13. Ren: I will tell the team which examinations remain incomplete in the electronic workflow.
  14. Dev: A clear status prevents an unfinished electronic step from being mistaken for an examination that never occurred.
  15. Ren: When the system is restored, someone needs to check that the records match rather than assume the outage fixed itself.
  16. Dev: Assign reconciliation ownership and follow the actual review process.
  17. Ren: That review should also check for duplicate entries and any information still missing.
  18. Dev: Exactly. Duplicate prevention is part of maintaining a trustworthy record.
  19. Ren: I will report the affected workflow, use the approved process, and keep unresolved items visible.
  20. Dev: Good. Restoration of system access is not proof that every examination record has been correctly reconciled.
Access is restored, reconciliation is not complete

The worklist becomes available again. Two downtime records still need verification against the restored system. No duplicates have been ruled out yet.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Technologist: "System access is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. restored

The worklist is available again in the supplied update.

2. Lead: "Two records still need ___."

Answer and explanation

D. verification

Two downtime records have not yet been checked against the restored system.

3. Technologist: "Reconciliation is not ___."

Answer and explanation

C. complete

Restored access does not establish that all records have been reconciled.

4. Lead: "Possible duplicates remain ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unchecked

The scenario states that duplicate records have not yet been ruled out.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.
